<details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_01QbpvUTQk1gLLGVwAgEkbyR`</summary>

```text
1	\begin{abstract}
2	When LLM-generated annotations feed into causal inference, annotation quality is the wrong thing to worry about; the causal DAG topology determines whether errors attenuate, amplify, or reverse the treatment effect estimate. We derive bias expressions for seven canonical topologies under $K$-class non-differential misclassification in linear structural equation models and show that $K {\geq} 3$ introduces a structural possibility of sign reversal absent in the binary case. Under proportional effects, five topologies preserve the treatment effect sign under regularity conditions; exposure and IV can reverse it. Under empirical LLM confusion matrices, sign reversal is confined to exposure topology. We introduce Annotation Sensitivity Values (ASVs), with proved ($K{=}2$, $K{=}3$ symmetric) and extensively validated (general $K{\geq}3$) monotonicity that yields a vulnerability ranking from IV (maximally fragile) to M-bias (immune), and show that matrix-based corrections are provably ineffective under linear regression for four topologies due to Frisch--Waugh--Lovell invariance. A case study with 10 real LLM confusion matrices confirms: topology-aware correction reduces exposure bias from 61\% to $<$1\%, while topology-blind corrections are provably futile under linear regression for four topologies.
3	\end{abstract}
4	
```

```text
1	\section{Related Work}
2	\label{sec:related_work}
3	
4	\paragraph{Measurement error in causal inference.}
5	Foundations appear in \citet{carroll2006, gustafson2004, buonaccorsi2010, bound2001measurement}, with DAG-based formalization by \citet{hernan_cole2009} and \citet{kuroki_pearl2014}. For binary variables, non-differential misclassification biases toward the null~\cite{bross1954misclassification, brenner1993, ogburn_vanderweele2012}, but this fails for polytomous exposures~\cite{dosemeci1990, jurek2005, veirod_laake2001}. Extensions address differential error~\cite{edwards_lessler_naimi2021, imai_yamamoto2010}, mismeasured covariates~\cite{shu_yi2019a, shu_yi2019b}, mediation~\cite{valeri_vanderweele2013}, and nonclassical error~\cite{schennach2020mismeasured, nguimkeu_denteh_tchernis2019, wei2025multiply}. All analyze a single DAG; ours characterizes how the same confusion matrix produces qualitatively different bias across seven topologies.
6	
7	\paragraph{Text classifiers and LLM annotation.}
8	\citet{grimmer2022} and \citet{feder_etal2022} provide foundations for text-as-data causal inference. \citet{wood_doughty2018} first applied DAG reasoning to classifier error; \citet{teblunthuis2024} gave the most complete binary treatment ($K{=}2$, four DAGs); \citet{lee_wood_doughty2024} extended to categorical LLM classifiers but covered only confounding. On annotation quality, \citet{gilardi2023chatgpt} and \citet{ziems2024can} document rapid LLM adoption~\cite{tan2024llm}, \citet{baumann2025} found 31\% incorrect conclusions, and \citet{kim_garg2025} showed 60\% error correlation~\cite{pangakis_wolken_fasching2023, cheng_mayya_sedoc2024, audinet2025, keith_jensen_oconnor2020, variance_aware_llm2026, hidden_measurement_error2026}. These works either restrict to binary labels, cover a single topology, or diagnose quality without connecting it to causal validity.
9	
10	\paragraph{Sensitivity analysis and correction methods.}
11	Our ASV framework draws on E-values~\cite{vanderweele_ding2017, mathur2018}, robustness values~\cite{cinelli2020making}, and mediation sensitivity~\cite{imai_keele_yamamoto2010, ding_vanderweele2016, pryzant_etal2021}, but parametrizes annotation error severity rather than omitted variable strength. \citet{lash2009}, \citet{blackwell_honaker_king2017}, \citet{mahajan2006}, and \citet{hu2008} provide complementary frameworks for misclassified regressors. For correction, PPI++~\cite{angelopoulos2023} and DSL~\cite{egami_hinck_stewart_wei2023} are topology-agnostic~\cite{zrnic_candes2024, demirel2024prediction, cadei2025, gligoric2025}. We show this agnosticism is costly: correction effectiveness is topology-dependent, with matrix-based methods provably ineffective under FWL decomposition for four topologies.
12	
```

```text
1	\section{Introduction}
2	\label{sec:introduction}
3	
4	Large language models are rapidly replacing human annotators in computational social science~\cite{gilardi2023chatgpt, ziems2024can, egami_hinck_stewart_wei2023, baumann2025}. Most tasks are inherently multi-class (stance detection: 3--4 categories, sentiment: 5, disaster response: 10+), and annotation errors propagate into downstream causal estimates in ways that depend on both error structure and causal design. \citet{baumann2025} show that 31\% of hypotheses yield incorrect conclusions with state-of-the-art LLMs, with 60\% error correlation across models~\cite{kim_garg2025}. Standard metrics do not capture this risk: within $F_1$ 0.61--0.69, causal bias varies by an order of magnitude across topologies.
5	
6	The transition at $K{\geq}3$ is not gradual but structural. For $K{=}2$, the stochastic constraint forces any confusion matrix with positive diagonal to preserve coefficient signs, an algebraic property of the $2{\times}2$ matrix dimension~\cite{brenner1993}. This guarantee vanishes at $K{\geq}3$: the higher-dimensional confusion matrix admits negative effective weights on categories with opposite-sign coefficients; sign reversal can occur even under high accuracy~\cite{veirod_laake2001}. Under a uniform Dirichlet prior, 83.4\% of $K{=}3$ exposure confusion matrices produce sign reversal of at least one secondary coefficient; under realistic accuracy (diagonal $\geq 0.7$), 2.1\% still do. The primary treatment coefficient is never reversed under the tested $\beta$ configurations where a single category dominates ($\beta_1 \gg \beta_2$), an empirical regularity we discuss in Section~\ref{sec:method}.
7	
8	$F_1$ does not exhibit significant rank correlation with causal bias ($\rho = -0.10$, $p = 0.45$ across 56 HumAID scenarios; bias 4.1--42.1\% within $F_1$ 0.614--0.690). Prior work noted polytomous sign reversal~\cite{dosemeci1990, veirod_laake2001} and confounder amplification~\cite{brenner1993}, but covered at most one topology~\cite{lee_wood_doughty2024}. We present the first systematic analysis jointly addressing multi-class ($K{\geq}3$) misclassification across seven DAG topologies, with analytical predictions matching Monte Carlo within 1.6\% MAPE across 161 scenarios.
9	
10	We address these gaps with three contributions:
11	
12	\begin{enumerate}
13	\item \textbf{Multi-class bias taxonomy.} We derive bias expressions for seven canonical DAG topologies\footnote{These span the four structural roles of the annotated variable: treatment (exposure), outcome-side covariate (confounding, mediation, M-bias, front-door), selection variable (collider), and instrument (IV).} (Figure~\ref{fig:dag_taxonomy}) under $K$-class non-differential misclassification and establish a sign-preservation/reversal dichotomy: five topologies preserve the treatment effect sign, while exposure and IV can reverse it.
14	
15	\item \textbf{Annotation Sensitivity Values.} We define three ASV diagnostics (magnitude, significance, and sign-flip) with proved envelope monotonicity (Theorems~\ref{thm:monotone},~\ref{thm:k3_symmetric}) and $\varepsilon$-approximate monotonicity for general $K{\geq}3$ (Conjecture~\ref{conj:monotone}), revealing a vulnerability spectrum from IV (maximally fragile) to M-bias (immune).
16	
17	\item \textbf{Topology-aware correction routing.} FWL invariance renders matrix-based corrections provably ineffective for four covariate topologies. We evaluate five correction methods and provide a topology-aware decision procedure (Algorithm~\ref{alg:correction}, Appendix), validated with real LLM confusion matrices.
18	\end{enumerate}
19	
```

```text
1	\section{Method}
2	\label{sec:method}
3	
4	\subsection{Setup and Notation}
5	\label{sec:notation}
6	
7	Let $A$ denote a true $K$-class categorical variable (e.g., sentiment with $K{=}3$ levels) and $A^*$ its observed, potentially misclassified version produced by an LLM annotator. The misclassification process is characterized by a $K{\times}K$ confusion matrix $C$ with entries $C_{jk} = P(A^*{=}j \mid A{=}k)$, where each column sums to one (column-stochastic: each column gives the distribution of predicted labels for a given true label). Following \citet{hernan_cole2009}, we represent this measurement process using \emph{starred-node} DAGs: the true variable $A$ is latent, and the observed $A^*$ is connected to $A$ via a dashed measurement edge.
8	
9	\paragraph{Notation conventions.}
10	Our analysis characterizes probability limits ($N \to \infty$) under the FWL theorem~\cite{frisch1933partial}, which explains why matrix-based corrections fail for covariate topologies (Section~\ref{sec:correction}). Notation is collected in Table~\ref{tab:notation} (Appendix~\ref{app:notation_table}).
11	
12	\begin{definition}[Non-differential misclassification]
13	\label{def:nondiff}
14	Misclassification of $A$ is non-differential with respect to outcome $Y$ and covariates $\mathbf{Z}$ if $P(A^*{=}j \mid A{=}k, Y, \mathbf{Z}) = P(A^*{=}j \mid A{=}k) = C_{jk}$ for all $j,k$.
15	\end{definition}
16	
17	In batch annotation pipelines, the LLM processes each text independently without access to $Y$, making non-differentiality an architectural invariant~\cite{ziems2024can}; Y-stratified permutation tests confirm this (1/12 rejection at $\alpha{=}0.05$; Appendix~\ref{sec:y_stratified_test}). Non-differentiality may be violated when $Y$ correlates with text content, in which case our Y-stratified test provides empirical verification. Under stress testing, sign reversal occurs in 14/16 $K{\geq}3$ exposure configurations at $\eta{=}0.01$ structured departure (Appendix~\ref{sec:nondiff_stress}).
18	
19	Throughout, $\tau$ denotes the causal estimand of interest, whose definition varies by topology: the confounder's coefficient, the treatment effect, the indirect effect, or the Wald ratio (Appendix~\ref{app:confounding}ff.).
20	
21	We study seven canonical DAG topologies (Figure~\ref{fig:dag_taxonomy})~\cite{pearl2009, hernan2020causal}, deriving probability limits under linear SEMs~\cite{carroll2006}; logistic regression robustness is verified in Appendix~\ref{sec:logistic_dgp} and~\ref{sec:fwl_nonlinear}.
22	
23	\subsection{Multi-Class Bias Expressions}
24	\label{sec:bias_expressions}
25	
26	For each topology, we derive the $\operatorname{plim}$\footnote{The probability limit (plim) is the value to which an estimator converges as sample size $N \to \infty$; it characterizes systematic bias separately from sampling variability.} of the naive estimator that substitutes $A^*$ for $A$. Let $\tau$ denote the target causal parameter, whose precise definition is topology-specific (e.g., the coefficient on $X$ for confounding, the category-specific $\beta_j$ for exposure, or the direct effect $\tau_{\text{direct}}$ for mediation), and $\hat{\tau}(A^*)$ the estimator using misclassified annotations.
27	
28	\paragraph{Confounding ($A$ = confounder).}
29	When $A$ confounds the $X{\to}Y$ relationship, the OLS estimator controlling for $A^*$ has probability limit
30	\begin{equation}
31	\label{eq:confounding}
32	\operatorname{plim}\;\hat{\tau}(A^*) = \bigl(\mathbb{E}[Z^{\prime}Z]\bigr)^{-1}\mathbb{E}[Z^{\prime}X]\,\boldsymbol{\beta},
33	\end{equation}
34	where $Z = [X, \text{dummies}(A^*)]$, $\boldsymbol{\beta}$ contains the true coefficients, and the target parameter $\tau$ is the coefficient on $X$. Misclassification produces residual confounding that monotonically approaches the omitted variable bias, but the sign of $\hat{\tau}$ is preserved: $\tau_{\text{sign\_flip}} = 0$ structurally.\footnote{Consistent with \citet{brenner1993}: misclassification degrades confounding control, producing amplification ($|\hat{\tau}| > |\tau|$, universal in this topology) but never sign reversal ($\operatorname{sign}(\hat{\tau}) \neq \operatorname{sign}(\tau)$).}
35	
36	\paragraph{Exposure ($A$ = treatment variable).}
37	When $A$ is the exposure of interest, the biased coefficient for category $j$ becomes a weighted combination of true coefficients:
38	\begin{equation}
39	\label{eq:exposure}
40	\operatorname{plim}\;\hat{\beta}_j(A^*) = \sum_{k=1}^{K} w_{jk}\,\beta_k,
41	\end{equation}
42	where $w_{jk}$ depends on the confusion matrix $C$ and the population marginal $\pi_k = P(A{=}k)$. Unlike binary misclassification~\cite{brenner1993}, $K{\geq}3$ permits $w_{jk}$ to assign negative weight to categories with opposite-sign coefficients, producing \emph{sign reversal} (83.4\% under uniform Dirichlet prior); this is structurally impossible at $K{=}2$.
43	
44	\begin{figure*}[t]
45	\centering
46	\includegraphics[width=\textwidth]{fig2_bias_distribution.pdf}
47	\caption{Bias distributions under 100{,}000 random $K{=}3$ confusion matrices (uniform Dirichlet; realistic-accuracy results in Section~\ref{sec:taxonomy_results}). Red = sign reversal. Exposure: 83.4\% sign reversal; IV: 52.6\%.}
48	\label{fig:bias_distribution}
49	\end{figure*}
50	
51	\paragraph{Numerical Verification 2: Primary coefficient preservation.}
52	Across 23 empirical LLM configurations, the primary coefficient (the category with the largest true effect $|\beta_k|$) never reverses sign, while 61\% reverse a secondary (non-dominant) coefficient, confirmed under 100{,}000 Dirichlet matrices with diagonal ${\geq}\,0.7$ across six beta-ratio conditions (1.0 to 50.0) with 0\% primary sign-flip (Appendix~\ref{app:exposure}).
53	
54	\paragraph{Mediation ($A$ = mediator).}
55	When $A$ mediates the $X{\to}Y$ relationship~\cite{vanderweele2015explanation}, the direct effect estimator controlling for $A^*$ has probability limit
56	\begin{equation}
57	\label{eq:mediation}
58	\operatorname{plim}\;\hat{\tau}_{\text{direct}}(A^*) = \tau_{\text{direct}} + \bigl(1 - \lambda(C)\bigr)\,\tau_{\text{indirect}},
59	\end{equation}
60	where $\lambda(C) \in [0,1]$ under proportional effects (Appendix~\ref{app:proof}, Proposition~\ref{prop:lambda_bound}), with $\lambda(I) = 1$ under perfect classification. Misclassification ($\lambda < 1$) causes the residual indirect effect to be absorbed into the direct path estimate, producing upward bias in $|\hat{\tau}_{\text{direct}}|$ but preserving the sign ($\tau_{\text{sign\_flip}} = 0$).
61	
62	\paragraph{Collider, M-bias, and front-door.}
63	Three topologies exhibit protective or immune behavior. \textbf{Collider:} conditioning on $A^*$ instead of $A$ attenuates the spurious path, so annotation error reduces collider bias ($\tau_{\text{sign\_flip}} = 0$). \textbf{M-bias:} in the M-shaped DAG~\cite{greenland2003}, misclassifying $A$ weakens the M-bias path; this topology is immune ($\text{ASV} = \infty$). \textbf{Front-door:} the front-door estimator~\cite{bellemare_bloem2024} is attenuated in both $X \to A$ and $A \to Y$ stages, producing moderate bias with $\tau_{\text{sign\_flip}} = 0$.
64	
65	\paragraph{Instrumental variable ($A$ = instrument).}
66	The Wald estimator~\cite{angrist_imbens1995} $\hat{\tau}_{\text{IV}} = \hat{\text{cov}}(A^*, Y) / \hat{\text{cov}}(A^*, X)$ has $C$ in the denominator. Misclassification can drive first-stage coefficients toward zero or reverse their sign; under random Dirichlet matrices, 52.6\% produce sign reversal; IV is the most fragile topology (Appendix~\ref{sec:iv_dirichlet_subspace}).
67	
68	\paragraph{Structural sign-flip guarantee.}
69	\begin{proposition}[Sign preservation: analytic results]
70	\label{prop:signflip}
71	Under non-differential misclassification with $C_{kk} > 0$ for all $k$:
72	\emph{(a)}~Confounding preserves sign when $\operatorname{sign}(\tau) = \operatorname{sign}(\tau {+} B_{\mathrm{OV}})$, where $B_{\mathrm{OV}} {=} \operatorname{Cov}(g(A),f(A))/\operatorname{Var}(T)$ is the omitted-variable bias (Cauchy--Schwarz; Appendix~\ref{app:confounding}).
73	\emph{(b)}~Collider preserves sign when $|\tau| > \sqrt{\operatorname{Var}(g)\operatorname{Var}(h)}/\mathbb{E}[\operatorname{Var}(T{\mid}A)]$ (Berkson attenuation; Appendix~\ref{app:collider}); empirically verified across 100{,}000 matrices with zero violations.
74	\emph{(c)}~Exposure can produce sign reversal for $K {\geq} 3$, even with $C_{kk} {>} 0.5$ (counterexample in Appendix~\ref{app:exposure}).
75	\end{proposition}
76	
77	Sign preservation for non-exposure topologies holds under topology-specific conditions (Table~\ref{tab:sign_conditions}, Appendix~\ref{app:conditions}), verified across 100{,}000 random matrices with zero violations; analytic proofs for general $K$ remain open.
78	
79	\subsection{Annotation Sensitivity Values}
80	\label{sec:asv}
81	
82	We adapt the sensitivity analysis paradigm (E-values~\cite{vanderweele_ding2017}, partial-$R^2$ bounds~\cite{cinelli2020making}, coefficient stability~\cite{oster2019}) to $K{\times}K$ annotation error and define Annotation Sensitivity Values (ASVs): we prove $\varepsilon$-approximate monotonicity along $C(\delta)$ (Theorem~\ref{thm:monotone}), which reduces $K^2$ dimensions to a scalar; define three ASV diagnostics for topology-dependent failure modes; and combine ASVs with FWL invariance to route corrections.
83	
84	\paragraph{Parameterization.}
85	We set $C(\delta) = (1{-}\delta)I + \delta C_0$, where $C_0$ is a reference confusion matrix and $\delta \in [0,1]$ controls severity ($\delta{=}0$: perfect; $\delta{=}1$: matches $C_0$). This linear path is one of many in the column-stochastic polytope from $I$ to $C_0$; ASV thresholds are path-dependent, but our bootstrap procedure (Section~\ref{sec:asv_results}) captures estimation uncertainty along the empirical path. We recommend bootstrapping $C_0$ and reporting ASV confidence intervals (Appendix~\ref{sec:bootstrap_asv}).
86	\paragraph{Three ASV types.}
87	We define three complementary measures: (1)~\textbf{Magnitude ASV} $\delta^*_{\text{mag}} = \min\{\delta : |\operatorname{plim}\;\hat{\tau}(C(\delta)) - \tau| > \epsilon\}$, analogous to E-values; (2)~\textbf{Significance ASV} $\delta^*_{\text{sig}} = \min\{\delta : |\operatorname{plim}\;\hat{\tau}(C(\delta))| < z_{\alpha/2} \cdot SE(N)\}$, relevant for attenuating topologies; (3)~\textbf{Sign-flip ASV} $\delta^*_{\text{sf}} = \min\{\delta : \operatorname{sign}(\hat{\tau}) \neq \operatorname{sign}(\tau)\}$, for exposure and IV only. The significance ASV depends on $N$ through $SE(N)$, exhibiting a sharp phase transition at a critical $N^*$ below which all rejections are blocked.
88	
89	\begin{theorem}[Envelope monotonicity, $K{=}2$]
90	\label{thm:monotone}
91	For $K{=}2$ confounding and mediation topologies with proportional effects ($f = \lambda g$ for some scalar $\lambda$) under $C(\delta) = (1{-}\delta)I + \delta C_0$ with $(C_0)_{jj} \geq \tfrac{1}{2}$, $|B(C(\delta))|$ is monotonically non-decreasing in $\delta \in [0,1]$.
92	\end{theorem}
93	
94	The proof (Appendix~\ref{app:monotonicity_proof},~\ref{par:med_monotone}) exploits perspective function convexity. Proportional effects ($f{=}\lambda g$) is plausible when overall annotation competence drives both paths; all 23 empirical configurations preserve sign, with 97.3\% under non-proportional effects (Appendix~\ref{sec:proportional_effects}).
95	
96	\begin{theorem}[$K{=}3$ symmetric monotonicity]
97	\label{thm:k3_symmetric}
98	For $K{=}3$ with symmetric confusion matrix $C_{jk} = c$ for $j \neq k$ and $C_{kk} = 1{-}2c$, $c \in (0, 1/3)$, and proportional effects ($f{=}\lambda g$), envelope monotonicity holds exactly: $|B(C(\delta))|$ is strictly monotone in $\delta$ for confounding, mediation, and collider topologies whenever $\mathrm{Var}(g) > 0$.
99	\end{theorem}
100	
101	Theorem~\ref{thm:k3_symmetric} anchors exact monotonicity at $K{=}3$; for general asymmetric matrices ($d_{\mathrm{rel}}{=}0.33$; Remark~\ref{rem:symmetry}), Conjecture~\ref{conj:monotone} provides $\varepsilon < 0.017$ coverage. The proof exploits the reduction $s = c\delta$ (Appendix~\ref{app:k3_symmetric_proof}).
102	
103	\paragraph{From proofs to numerical evidence.}
104	Exact monotonicity (Theorems~\ref{thm:monotone}--\ref{thm:k3_symmetric}) does not extend to general $K{\geq}3$ column-stochastic structures; we state a conjecture supported by numerical evidence:
105	
106	\begin{conjecture}[$\varepsilon$-approximate envelope monotonicity]
107	\label{conj:monotone}
108	For general $K{\geq}3$ non-IV topologies under $C(\delta) = (1{-}\delta)I + \delta C_0$, $|B(C(\delta))|$ is $\varepsilon$-approximately monotonic with $\varepsilon < 0.02$ for $|\beta| \leq 1$, scaling with effect magnitude (Table~\ref{tab:conjecture_verify},~\ref{tab:dgp_sensitivity}; Appendix~\ref{sec:conjecture_verify}). The safe/fragile partition (five sign-preserving topologies versus exposure and IV) is robust across all tested parameterizations.
109	\end{conjecture}
110	
111	Numerical support spans 100{,}000 Dirichlet matrices and 545{,}945 adversarial curves (Appendix~\ref{app:adversarial_monotonicity}).
112	
113	\begin{remark}[ASV and E-values as complementary tools]
114	\label{rem:evalue}
115	ASV and E-values~\cite{vanderweele_ding2017} address orthogonal sources (annotation error vs.\ unmeasured confounding) and are most valuable jointly (Table~\ref{tab:sensitivity_comparison}, Appendix). ASV is topology-dependent and targets sign-flip failure modes absent from confounding analysis.
116	\end{remark}
117	
118	Under alternating-sign effects ($\boldsymbol{\beta}{=}[0,\,1.0,\,-0.5]$), magnitude ASV ranges from 0.09 (exposure) to $\infty$ (M-bias), a $4{\times}$ difference determined by topology (Table~\ref{tab:asv_stability}). Sign-flip ASV $< 1$ flags sign-reversal-prone pairs; magnitude ASV $< \delta_{\text{observed}}$ indicates bias exceeds tolerance (Algorithm~\ref{alg:correction}); the safe/fragile partition is stable across parameterizations ($\varepsilon < 0.02$; Appendix~\ref{sec:asv_stability}).
119	
```

        "content": "\\section{Experiments}\n\\label{sec:experiments}\n\nWe validate through Monte Carlo simulation ($K{=}3$), cross-$K$ robustness checks ($K{=}5,7$), and empirical validation with real LLM confusion matrices from three NLP tasks ($K{=}2,3,10$).\n\n\\subsection{Setup}\n\\label{sec:setup}\n\nFor each topology, we sample 100{,}000 confusion matrices from a Dirichlet$(1,1,1)$ prior, compute analytical probability limits, and validate against Monte Carlo simulation ($N{=}10{,}000$). ASV experiments use $C(\\delta) = (1{-}\\delta)I + \\delta C_0$.\n\n\\subsection{Bias Taxonomy}\n\\label{sec:taxonomy_results}\n\n\\paragraph{Theory-MC agreement.}\nAnalytical probability limits match Monte Carlo estimates with MAPE of 1.6\\%, averaged across 161 scenarios (23 LLM configurations $\\times$ 7 topologies; per-topology range: 0.3\\%--3.7\\%).\n\n\\paragraph{Exposure DAG: sign reversal under multi-class misclassification.}\nUnder realistic accuracy (diagonal $\\geq 0.7$), the violation rate is 13.7\\% with 2.1\\% sign reversal; at diagonal $\\geq 0.9$, violation persists at 11.9\\% (Appendix~\\ref{sec:iv_dirichlet_subspace}). Under the full Dirichlet prior, 85\\% of matrices violate the binary ``toward-null'' guarantee and 83.4\\% cause sign reversal; $\\min_k C_{kk} < 0.55$ predicts sign reversal with 91\\% accuracy (Appendix~\\ref{sec:sign_determinants}). The $K{=}2$ control confirms classical attenuation~\\cite{brenner1993}, establishing the multi-class failure as structurally distinct.\n\n\\paragraph{Structural guarantee for non-exposure topologies.}\nFive topologies exhibit zero sign-flip rate (Table~\\ref{tab:taxonomy}): confounding, mediation, and front-door show 100\\% amplification~\\cite{brenner1993, dosemeci1990} but never reverse the sign; collider is protective ($|B(C)|$ non-increasing; Conjecture~\\ref{conj:monotone}); M-bias shows weakest sensitivity (53.2\\% violation). All patterns persist at $K{=}5,7$ (Appendix~\\ref{sec:cross_k_robustness}).\n\n\\paragraph{IV: Wald estimator instability.}\nThe IV topology is the sole non-exposure exception: 52.6\\% of random matrices cause sign reversal, stable across $K{=}3,5,7$. Diagonal dominance ($C_{kk} > 0.5$) eliminates sign reversal but preserves amplification (Appendix~\\ref{sec:iv_dirichlet_subspace}); however, all 23 empirical LLM configurations show zero IV sign reversal.\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\caption{Bias under $K{=}3$ non-differential misclassification (100{,}000 Dirichlet$(1,1,1)$ matrices). Violation: $|\\text{bias}| > 0$; Sign Flip: any coefficient reverses sign; Amplif.: $|\\hat{\\tau}| > |\\tau|$. $\\tau_{\\text{sf}}{=}0$?: sign-flip guarantee holds unconditionally (Yes), conditionally (Cond.; Proposition~\\ref{prop:signflip}), or not (No). Collider 100\\% amplification reflects Berkson bias; misclassification is protective.}\n\\label{tab:taxonomy}\n\\small\n\\resizebox{\\columnwidth}{!}{\\begin{tabular}{lcccc}\n\\toprule\n\\textbf{Topology} & \\textbf{Violation} & \\textbf{Sign Flip} & \\textbf{Amplif.} & $\\tau_{\\text{sf}}{=}0$? \\\\\n\\midrule\nConfounding & 100\\% & 0\\% & 100\\% & Cond. \\\\\nExposure & 85\\% & 83.4\\% & 14.1\\% & No \\\\\nMediation & 100\\% & 0\\% & 100\\% & Cond. \\\\\nFront-door & 100\\% & 0\\% & 100\\% & Cond. \\\\\nCollider & 100\\% & 0\\% & 100\\% & Cond. \\\\\nM-bias & 53.2\\% & 0\\% & 10.4\\% & Yes \\\\\nIV & 100\\% & 52.6\\% & 100\\% & No \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}}\n\\end{table}\n\nThe bias distributions are visualized in Figure~\\ref{fig:bias_distribution}.\n\n\\subsection{ASV Framework Validation}\n\\label{sec:asv_results}\n\n\\begin{figure*}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=0.85\\textwidth]{fig3_asv_ranking.pdf}\n\\caption{ASV vulnerability ranking across topologies. Three ASV types (magnitude, significance, sign-flip) reveal a topology-dependent spectrum from IV (maximally fragile) to M-bias (immune, ASV $= \\infty$).}\n\\label{fig:asv_ranking}\n\\end{figure*}\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\caption{ASV summary across topologies ($K{=}3$, $\\tau{=}0.5$). ---: not applicable.}\n\\label{tab:asv_summary}\n\\begin{tabular}{lccc}\n\\toprule\n\\textbf{Topology} & \\textbf{Mag ASV} & \\textbf{Power} & \\textbf{Sign-flip} \\\\\n\\midrule\nIV          & 0.10--0.61 & 0\\%   & 1.1--6.7 \\\\\nFront-door  & 0.06--0.39 & 100\\% & --- \\\\\nConfounding & 0.08--0.49 & 100\\% & --- \\\\\nMediation   & 0.08--0.45 & 0\\%   & --- \\\\\nCollider    & 0.34--2.03 & 0\\%   & --- \\\\\nM-bias      & $\\infty$   & 98\\%  & --- \\\\\nExposure    & ---        & ---   & 83.4\\% \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n\n\\paragraph{ASV results.}\nThe ASV envelope is $\\varepsilon$-approximately monotonic for non-IV topologies ($\\varepsilon < 0.02$ for non-exposure under moderate effects, larger for exposure; Conjecture~\\ref{conj:monotone}). Figure~\\ref{fig:asv_ranking} shows the vulnerability ranking: IV is maximally fragile, M-bias is immune ($\\text{ASV} = \\infty$). Sign-flip ASV: 83.4\\% of exposure matrices yield $\\delta^*_{\\text{sf}} < 1$; IV ranges 1.1--6.7 (Table~\\ref{tab:asv_summary}). Significance ASV exhibits a phase transition at $N^* \\approx 6{,}000$--$10{,}000$; bootstrap CIs ($B{=}1{,}000$; Appendix~\\ref{sec:bootstrap_asv}) confirm stable topology ranking despite wide IV intervals.\n\n\\subsection{Empirical Validation}\n\\label{sec:empirical}\n\nWe validate using real LLM confusion matrices from HumAID~\\cite{alam2021humaid} ($K{=}10$), VAST ($K{=}3$), and CivilComments ($K{=}2$): four LLMs, 23 configurations, 161 bias scenarios.\n\n\\paragraph{Empirical bias ranking.}\nHumAID ($K{=}10$) matches predictions: IV (12.4--42.1\\%) and exposure (9.0--17.4\\%) dominate; remaining topologies $<$15\\%. $F_1$ does not exhibit significant rank correlation with bias ($\\rho = -0.10$, $p = 0.45$; Figure~\\ref{fig:f1_vs_bias}), but per-topology patterns diverge ($\\rho \\leq -0.53$ for five topologies vs.\\ $0.87$ for M-bias; Appendix~\\ref{sec:within_topo_f1}). Of exposure coefficients, 14/23 (61\\%) exhibit sign reversal, all secondary; zero across 138 non-exposure scenarios.\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{fig5_f1_vs_bias.pdf}\n\\caption{Topology determines downstream causal bias, not $F_1$. HumAID $K{=}10$ (56 points): within $F_1$ range 0.614--0.690, bias varies $10{\\times}$ (IV 42.1\\% vs.\\ collider $<$5.6\\%).}\n\\label{fig:f1_vs_bias}\n\\end{figure}\n\n\n\\subsection{Correction Guidance}\n\\label{sec:correction}\n\nWe evaluate five correction methods (naive plug-in, MLA, DSL~\\cite{egami_hinck_stewart_wei2023}, MC-SIMEX~\\cite{kuchenhoff2006simex}, PPI++~\\cite{angelopoulos2023}) across all topologies (210 configurations; Table~\\ref{tab:correction}, Appendix~\\ref{app:supplementary}). Regression calibration~\\cite{carroll2006} is designed for continuous measurement error and does not apply to categorical misclassification.\n\n\\paragraph{FWL invariance.}\nUnder linear regression, FWL invariance renders matrix-based methods (naive plug-in, MLA) provably ineffective for covariate topologies, achieving 0\\% bias reduction (Appendix~\\ref{app:fwl_proof}); under logistic regression this breaks but ranking persists ($|\\Delta| < 0.02$; Appendix~\\ref{sec:fwl_nonlinear}). To our knowledge, this futility result (that matrix-based corrections cannot reduce covariate-induced bias under FWL decomposition) has not been explicitly stated in the measurement error literature~\\cite{carroll2006, buonaccorsi2010}.\n\n\\paragraph{Method ranking.}\nPPI++ achieves the highest RMSE reduction (47.0\\% at $K{=}3$) but requires gold labels and harms M-bias ($-$88\\%); MC-SIMEX is gold-free and $K$-robust (27--70\\%), though it harms front-door ($-$62.4\\%); rankings are stable across $K$ ($\\rho \\geq 0.80$; Appendix~\\ref{sec:correction_k_sensitivity}).\n\n\\paragraph{Correction decision procedure.}\nAlgorithm~\\ref{alg:correction} (Appendix) routes through three stages: risk assessment (exposure/IV high; M-bias low), method selection (PPI++ with gold, MC-SIMEX/DSL without), and FWL screening (skip plug-in/MLA for covariate topologies). Under topology uncertainty, ASV under candidate topologies provides worst-case sensitivity.\n\n\\paragraph{Case studies and sensitivity.}\nClinicalBERT and HumAID ($K{=}10$) confirm all patterns under linear and logistic models ($|\\Delta| < 0.02$; Appendix~\\ref{app:case_study},~\\ref{sec:correction_k_sensitivity}). End-to-end demos (Table~\\ref{tab:e2e-demo}; Appendix~\\ref{sec:real_cm_demo}) validate: PPI++ reduces exposure bias from 42.9\\% to 0.2\\%, confounding achieves 0\\% improvement (FWL), and topology-agnostic IV correction amplifies bias ($-$139\\%).\n",
        "numLines": 113,
        "startLine": 1,
        "totalLines": 113
      }
    },

        "content": "\\section{Conclusion}\n\\label{sec:conclusion}\n\nWe characterized how multi-class annotation errors interact with causal DAG topology: at $K{\\geq}3$, exposure and IV can reverse treatment effect signs while five topologies preserve them. ASVs provide a topology-dependent vulnerability ranking; matrix-based corrections are provably ineffective for four covariate topologies (FWL invariance). Practitioners should identify the topology first: for exposure, PPI++ reduces bias by 93.6\\%; for IV, all tested corrections worsen the estimate.\n\n\\section*{Limitations}\n\nOur closed-form results assume linear SEMs with proportional effects ($f{=}\\lambda g$); sign preservation empirically holds at 97.3\\% under non-proportional effects (Table~\\ref{tab:proportional_empirical}), but recommendations do not directly extend to nonlinear models (matching, doubly-robust). The framework assumes a known DAG with non-differential misclassification (Appendix~\\ref{sec:nondiff_stress}); DAG misspecification under measurement error may compound bias. ASV computation requires gold labels for $C^{-1}$; error propagation under small gold-label budgets ($<$500) and general $K{\\geq}3$ envelope monotonicity (Conjecture~\\ref{conj:monotone}) remain open.\n",

        "content": "\\section{Introduction}\n\\label{sec:introduction}\n\nLarge language models are rapidly replacing human annotators in computational social science~\\cite{gilardi2023chatgpt, ziems2024can, egami_hinck_stewart_wei2023, baumann2025}. Most tasks are inherently multi-class (stance detection: 3--4 categories, sentiment: 5, disaster response: 10+), and annotation errors propagate into downstream causal estimates in ways that depend on both error structure and causal design. \\citet{baumann2025} show that 31\\% of hypotheses yield incorrect conclusions with state-of-the-art LLMs, with 60\\% error correlation across models~\\cite{kim_garg2025}. Standard metrics do not capture this risk: within $F_1$ 0.61--0.69, causal bias varies by an order of magnitude across topologies.\n\nThe transition at $K{\\geq}3$ is not gradual but structural. For $K{=}2$, the stochastic constraint forces any confusion matrix with positive diagonal to preserve coefficient signs, an algebraic property of the $2{\\times}2$ matrix dimension~\\cite{brenner1993}. This guarantee vanishes at $K{\\geq}3$: the higher-dimensional confusion matrix admits negative effective weights on categories with opposite-sign coefficients; sign reversal can occur even under high accuracy~\\cite{veirod_laake2001}. Under a uniform Dirichlet prior, 83.4\\% of $K{=}3$ exposure confusion matrices produce sign reversal of at least one secondary coefficient; under realistic accuracy (diagonal $\\geq 0.7$), 2.1\\% still do. The primary treatment coefficient is never reversed under the tested $\\beta$ configurations where a single category dominates ($\\beta_1 \\gg \\beta_2$), an empirical regularity we discuss in Section~\\ref{sec:method}.\n\n$F_1$ does not exhibit significant rank correlation with causal bias ($\\rho = -0.10$, $p = 0.45$ across 56 HumAID scenarios; bias 4.1--42.1\\% within $F_1$ 0.614--0.690). Prior work noted polytomous sign reversal~\\cite{dosemeci1990, veirod_laake2001} and confounder amplification~\\cite{brenner1993}, but covered at most one topology~\\cite{lee_wood_doughty2024}. We present the first systematic analysis jointly addressing multi-class ($K{\\geq}3$) misclassification across seven DAG topologies, with analytical predictions matching Monte Carlo within 1.6\\% MAPE across 161 scenarios.\n\nWe address these gaps with three contributions:\n\n\\begin{enumerate}\n\\item \\textbf{Multi-class bias taxonomy.} We derive bias expressions for seven canonical DAG topologies\\footnote{These span the four structural roles of the annotated variable: treatment (exposure), outcome-side covariate (confounding, mediation, M-bias, front-door), selection variable (collider), and instrument (IV).} (Figure~\\ref{fig:dag_taxonomy}) under $K$-class non-differential misclassification and establish a sign-preservation/reversal dichotomy: five topologies preserve the treatment effect sign, while exposure and IV can reverse it.\n\n\\item \\textbf{Annotation Sensitivity Values.} We define three ASV diagnostics (magnitude, significance, and sign-flip) with proved envelope monotonicity (Theorems~\\ref{thm:monotone},~\\ref{thm:k3_symmetric}) and $\\varepsilon$-approximate monotonicity for general $K{\\geq}3$ (Conjecture~\\ref{conj:monotone}), revealing a vulnerability spectrum from IV (maximally fragile) to M-bias (immune).\n\n\\item \\textbf{Topology-aware correction routing.} FWL invariance renders matrix-based corrections provably ineffective for four covariate topologies. We evaluate five correction methods and provide a topology-aware decision procedure (Algorithm~\\ref{alg:correction}, Appendix), validated with real LLM confusion matrices.\n\\end{enumerate}\n",
        "numLines": 19,
        "startLine": 1,
        "totalLines": 19
      }
    },

{
  "parentUuid": "1a40e781-2b9a-4e4f-bd45-1adbefad480e",
  "isSidechain": false,
  "attachment": {
    "type": "file",

    "content": {
      "type": "text",
      "file": {
        "filePath": "/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-prod_0510/projects/causal_annot_error_attrib/docs/paper/method.tex",
        "content": "\\section{Method}\n\\label{sec:method}\n\n\\subsection{Setup and Notation}\n\\label{sec:notation}\n\nLet $A$ denote a true $K$-class categorical variable (e.g., sentiment with $K{=}3$ levels) and $A^*$ its observed, potentially misclassified version produced by an LLM annotator. The misclassification process is characterized by a $K{\\times}K$ confusion matrix $C$ with entries $C_{jk} = P(A^*{=}j \\mid A{=}k)$, where each column sums to one (column-stochastic: each column gives the distribution of predicted labels for a given true label). Following \\citet{hernan_cole2009}, we represent this measurement process using \\emph{starred-node} DAGs: the true variable $A$ is latent, and the observed $A^*$ is connected to $A$ via a dashed measurement edge.\n\n\\paragraph{Notation conventions.}\nOur analysis characterizes probability limits ($N \\to \\infty$) under the FWL theorem~\\cite{frisch1933partial}, which explains why matrix-based corrections fail for covariate topologies (Section~\\ref{sec:correction}). Notation is collected in Table~\\ref{tab:notation} (Appendix~\\ref{app:notation_table}).\n\n\\begin{definition}[Non-differential misclassification]\n\\label{def:nondiff}\nMisclassification of $A$ is non-differential with respect to outcome $Y$ and covariates $\\mathbf{Z}$ if $P(A^*{=}j \\mid A{=}k, Y, \\mathbf{Z}) = P(A^*{=}j \\mid A{=}k) = C_{jk}$ for all $j,k$.\n\\end{definition}\n\nIn batch annotation pipelines, the LLM processes each text independently without access to $Y$, making non-differentiality an architectural invariant~\\cite{ziems2024can}; Y-stratified permutation tests confirm this (1/12 rejection at $\\alpha{=}0.05$; Appendix~\\ref{sec:y_stratified_test}). Non-differentiality may be violated when $Y$ correlates with text content, in which case our Y-stratified test provides empirical verification. Under stress testing, sign reversal occurs in 14/16 $K{\\geq}3$ exposure configurations at $\\eta{=}0.01$ structured departure (Appendix~\\ref{sec:nondiff_stress}).\n\nThroughout, $\\tau$ denotes the causal estimand of interest, whose definition varies by topology: the confounder's coefficient, the treatment effect, the indirect effect, or the Wald ratio (Appendix~\\ref{app:confounding}ff.).\n\nWe study seven canonical DAG topologies (Figure~\\ref{fig:dag_taxonomy})~\\cite{pearl2009, hernan2020causal}, deriving probability limits under linear SEMs~\\cite{carroll2006}; logistic regression robustness is verified in Appendix~\\ref{sec:logistic_dgp} and~\\ref{sec:fwl_nonlinear}.\n\n\\subsection{Multi-Class Bias Expressions}\n\\label{sec:bias_expressions}\n\nFor each topology, we derive the $\\operatorname{plim}$\\footnote{The probability limit (plim) is the value to which an estimator converges as sample size $N \\to \\infty$; it characterizes systematic bias separately from sampling variability.} of the naive estimator that substitutes $A^*$ for $A$. Let $\\tau$ denote the target causal parameter, whose precise definition is topology-specific (e.g., the coefficient on $X$ for confounding, the category-specific $\\beta_j$ for exposure, or the direct effect $\\tau_{\\text{direct}}$ for mediation), and $\\hat{\\tau}(A^*)$ the estimator using misclassified annotations.\n\n\\paragraph{Confounding ($A$ = confounder).}\nWhen $A$ confounds the $X{\\to}Y$ relationship, the OLS estimator controlling for $A^*$ has probability limit\n\\begin{equation}\n\\label{eq:confounding}\n\\operatorname{plim}\\;\\hat{\\tau}(A^*) = \\bigl(\\mathbb{E}[Z^{\\prime}Z]\\bigr)^{-1}\\mathbb{E}[Z^{\\prime}X]\\,\\boldsymbol{\\beta},\n\\end{equation}\nwhere $Z = [X, \\text{dummies}(A^*)]$, $\\boldsymbol{\\beta}$ contains the true coefficients, and the target parameter $\\tau$ is the coefficient on $X$. Misclassification produces residual confounding that monotonically approaches the omitted variable bias, but the sign of $\\hat{\\tau}$ is preserved: $\\tau_{\\text{sign\\_flip}} = 0$ structurally.\\footnote{Consistent with \\citet{brenner1993}: misclassification degrades confounding control, producing amplification ($|\\hat{\\tau}| > |\\tau|$, universal in this topology) but never sign reversal ($\\operatorname{sign}(\\hat{\\tau}) \\neq \\operatorname{sign}(\\tau)$).}\n\n\\paragraph{Exposure ($A$ = treatment variable).}\nWhen $A$ is the exposure of interest, the biased coefficient for category $j$ becomes a weighted combination of true coefficients:\n\\begin{equation}\n\\label{eq:exposure}\n\\operatorname{plim}\\;\\hat{\\beta}_j(A^*) = \\sum_{k=1}^{K} w_{jk}\\,\\beta_k,\n\\end{equation}\nwhere $w_{jk}$ depends on the confusion matrix $C$ and the population marginal $\\pi_k = P(A{=}k)$. Unlike binary misclassification~\\cite{brenner1993}, $K{\\geq}3$ permits $w_{jk}$ to assign negative weight to categories with opposite-sign coefficients, producing \\emph{sign reversal} (83.4\\% under uniform Dirichlet prior); this is structurally impossible at $K{=}2$.\n\n\\begin{figure*}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\textwidth]{fig2_bias_distribution.pdf}\n\\caption{Bias distributions under 100{,}000 random $K{=}3$ confusion matrices (uniform Dirichlet; realistic-accuracy results in Section~\\ref{sec:taxonomy_results}). Red = sign reversal. Exposure: 83.4\\% sign reversal; IV: 52.6\\%.}\n\\label{fig:bias_distribution}\n\\end{figure*}\n\n\\paragraph{Numerical Verification 2: Primary coefficient preservation.}\nAcross 23 empirical LLM configurations, the primary coefficient (the category with the largest true effect $|\\beta_k|$) never reverses sign, while 61\\% reverse a secondary (non-dominant) coefficient, confirmed under 100{,}000 Dirichlet matrices with diagonal ${\\geq}\\,0.7$ across six beta-ratio conditions (1.0 to 50.0) with 0\\% primary sign-flip (Appendix~\\ref{app:exposure}).\n\n\\paragraph{Mediation ($A$ = mediator).}\nWhen $A$ mediates the $X{\\to}Y$ relationship~\\cite{vanderweele2015explanation}, the direct effect estimator controlling for $A^*$ has probability limit\n\\begin{equation}\n\\label{eq:mediation}\n\\operatorname{plim}\\;\\hat{\\tau}_{\\text{direct}}(A^*) = \\tau_{\\text{direct}} + \\bigl(1 - \\lambda(C)\\bigr)\\,\\tau_{\\text{indirect}},\n\\end{equation}\nwhere $\\lambda(C) \\in [0,1]$ under proportional effects (Appendix~\\ref{app:proof}, Proposition~\\ref{prop:lambda_bound}), with $\\lambda(I) = 1$ under perfect classification. Misclassification ($\\lambda < 1$) causes the residual indirect effect to be absorbed into the direct path estimate, producing upward bias in $|\\hat{\\tau}_{\\text{direct}}|$ but preserving the sign ($\\tau_{\\text{sign\\_flip}} = 0$).\n\n\\paragraph{Collider, M-bias, and front-door.}\nThree topologies exhibit protective or immune behavior. \\textbf{Collider:} conditioning on $A^*$ instead of $A$ attenuates the spurious path, so annotation error reduces collider bias ($\\tau_{\\text{sign\\_flip}} = 0$). \\textbf{M-bias:} in the M-shaped DAG~\\cite{greenland2003}, misclassifying $A$ weakens the M-bias path; this topology is immune ($\\text{ASV} = \\infty$). \\textbf{Front-door:} the front-door estimator~\\cite{bellemare_bloem2024} is attenuated in both $X \\to A$ and $A \\to Y$ stages, producing moderate bias with $\\tau_{\\text{sign\\_flip}} = 0$.\n\n\\paragraph{Instrumental variable ($A$ = instrument).}\nThe Wald estimator~\\cite{angrist_imbens1995} $\\hat{\\tau}_{\\text{IV}} = \\hat{\\text{cov}}(A^*, Y) / \\hat{\\text{cov}}(A^*, X)$ has $C$ in the denominator. Misclassification can drive first-stage coefficients toward zero or reverse their sign; under random Dirichlet matrices, 52.6\\% produce sign reversal; IV is the most fragile topology (Appendix~\\ref{sec:iv_dirichlet_subspace}).\n\n\\paragraph{Structural sign-flip guarantee.}\n\\begin{proposition}[Sign preservation: analytic results]\n\\label{prop:signflip}\nUnder non-differential misclassification with $C_{kk} > 0$ for all $k$:\n\\emph{(a)}~Confounding preserves sign when $\\operatorname{sign}(\\tau) = \\operatorname{sign}(\\tau {+} B_{\\mathrm{OV}})$, where $B_{\\mathrm{OV}} {=} \\operatorname{Cov}(g(A),f(A))/\\operatorname{Var}(T)$ is the omitted-variable bias (Cauchy--Schwarz; Appendix~\\ref{app:confounding}).\n\\emph{(b)}~Collider preserves sign when $|\\tau| > \\sqrt{\\operatorname{Var}(g)\\operatorname{Var}(h)}/\\mathbb{E}[\\operatorname{Var}(T{\\mid}A)]$ (Berkson attenuation; Appendix~\\ref{app:collider}); empirically verified across 100{,}000 matrices with zero violations.\n\\emph{(c)}~Exposure can produce sign reversal for $K {\\geq} 3$, even with $C_{kk} {>} 0.5$ (counterexample in Appendix~\\ref{app:exposure}).\n\\end{proposition}\n\nSign preservation for non-exposure topologies holds under topology-specific conditions (Table~\\ref{tab:sign_conditions}, Appendix~\\ref{app:conditions}), verified across 100{,}000 random matrices with zero violations; analytic proofs for general $K$ remain open.\n\n\\subsection{Annotation Sensitivity Values}\n\\label{sec:asv}\n\nWe adapt the sensitivity analysis paradigm (E-values~\\cite{vanderweele_ding2017}, partial-$R^2$ bounds~\\cite{cinelli2020making}, coefficient stability~\\cite{oster2019}) to $K{\\times}K$ annotation error and define Annotation Sensitivity Values (ASVs): we prove $\\varepsilon$-approximate monotonicity along $C(\\delta)$ (Theorem~\\ref{thm:monotone}), which reduces $K^2$ dimensions to a scalar; define three ASV diagnostics for topology-dependent failure modes; and combine ASVs with FWL invariance to route corrections.\n\n\\paragraph{Parameterization.}\nWe set $C(\\delta) = (1{-}\\delta)I + \\delta C_0$, where $C_0$ is a reference confusion matrix and $\\delta \\in [0,1]$ controls severity ($\\delta{=}0$: perfect; $\\delta{=}1$: matches $C_0$). This linear path is one of many in the column-stochastic polytope from $I$ to $C_0$; ASV thresholds are path-dependent, but our bootstrap procedure (Section~\\ref{sec:asv_results}) captures estimation uncertainty along the empirical path. We recommend bootstrapping $C_0$ and reporting ASV confidence intervals (Appendix~\\ref{sec:bootstrap_asv}).\n\\paragraph{Three ASV types.}\nWe define three complementary measures: (1)~\\textbf{Magnitude ASV} $\\delta^*_{\\text{mag}} = \\min\\{\\delta : |\\operatorname{plim}\\;\\hat{\\tau}(C(\\delta)) - \\tau| > \\epsilon\\}$, analogous to E-values; (2)~\\textbf{Significance ASV} $\\delta^*_{\\text{sig}} = \\min\\{\\delta : |\\operatorname{plim}\\;\\hat{\\tau}(C(\\delta))| < z_{\\alpha/2} \\cdot SE(N)\\}$, relevant for attenuating topologies; (3)~\\textbf{Sign-flip ASV} $\\delta^*_{\\text{sf}} = \\min\\{\\delta : \\operatorname{sign}(\\hat{\\tau}) \\neq \\operatorname{sign}(\\tau)\\}$, for exposure and IV only. The significance ASV depends on $N$ through $SE(N)$, exhibiting a sharp phase transition at a critical $N^*$ below which all rejections are blocked.\n\n\\begin{theorem}[Envelope monotonicity, $K{=}2$]\n\\label{thm:monotone}\nFor $K{=}2$ confounding and mediation topologies with proportional effects ($f = \\lambda g$ for some scalar $\\lambda$) under $C(\\delta) = (1{-}\\delta)I + \\delta C_0$ with $(C_0)_{jj} \\geq \\tfrac{1}{2}$, $|B(C(\\delta))|$ is monotonically non-decreasing in $\\delta \\in [0,1]$.\n\\end{theorem}\n\nThe proof (Appendix~\\ref{app:monotonicity_proof},~\\ref{par:med_monotone}) exploits perspective function convexity. Proportional effects ($f{=}\\lambda g$) is plausible when overall annotation competence drives both paths; all 23 empirical configurations preserve sign, with 97.3\\% under non-proportional effects (Appendix~\\ref{sec:proportional_effects}).\n\n\\begin{theorem}[$K{=}3$ symmetric monotonicity]\n\\label{thm:k3_symmetric}\nFor $K{=}3$ with symmetric confusion matrix $C_{jk} = c$ for $j \\neq k$ and $C_{kk} = 1{-}2c$, $c \\in (0, 1/3)$, and proportional effects ($f{=}\\lambda g$), envelope monotonicity holds exactly: $|B(C(\\delta))|$ is strictly monotone in $\\delta$ for confounding, mediation, and collider topologies whenever $\\mathrm{Var}(g) > 0$.\n\\end{theorem}\n\nTheorem~\\ref{thm:k3_symmetric} anchors exact monotonicity at $K{=}3$; for general asymmetric matrices ($d_{\\mathrm{rel}}{=}0.33$; Remark~\\ref{rem:symmetry}), Conjecture~\\ref{conj:monotone} provides $\\varepsilon < 0.017$ coverage. The proof exploits the reduction $s = c\\delta$ (Appendix~\\ref{app:k3_symmetric_proof}).\n\n\\paragraph{From proofs to numerical evidence.}\nExact monotonicity (Theorems~\\ref{thm:monotone}--\\ref{thm:k3_symmetric}) does not extend to general $K{\\geq}3$ column-stochastic structures; we state a conjecture supported by numerical evidence:\n\n\\begin{conjecture}[$\\varepsilon$-approximate envelope monotonicity]\n\\label{conj:monotone}\nFor general $K{\\geq}3$ non-IV topologies under $C(\\delta) = (1{-}\\delta)I + \\delta C_0$, $|B(C(\\delta))|$ is $\\varepsilon$-approximately monotonic with $\\varepsilon < 0.02$ for $|\\beta| \\leq 1$, scaling with effect magnitude (Table~\\ref{tab:conjecture_verify},~\\ref{tab:dgp_sensitivity}; Appendix~\\ref{sec:conjecture_verify}). The safe/fragile partition (five sign-preserving topologies versus exposure and IV) is robust across all tested parameterizations.\n\\end{conjecture}\n\nNumerical support spans 100{,}000 Dirichlet matrices and 545{,}945 adversarial curves (Appendix~\\ref{app:adversarial_monotonicity}).\n\n\\begin{remark}[ASV and E-values as complementary tools]\n\\label{rem:evalue}\nASV and E-values~\\cite{vanderweele_ding2017} address orthogonal sources (annotation error vs.\\ unmeasured confounding) and are most valuable jointly (Table~\\ref{tab:sensitivity_comparison}, Appendix). ASV is topology-dependent and targets sign-flip failure modes absent from confounding analysis.\n\\end{remark}\n\nUnder alternating-sign effects ($\\boldsymbol{\\beta}{=}[0,\\,1.0,\\,-0.5]$), magnitude ASV ranges from 0.09 (exposure) to $\\infty$ (M-bias), a $4{\\times}$ difference determined by topology (Table~\\ref{tab:asv_stability}). Sign-flip ASV $< 1$ flags sign-reversal-prone pairs; magnitude ASV $< \\delta_{\\text{observed}}$ indicates bias exceeds tolerance (Algorithm~\\ref{alg:correction}); the safe/fragile partition is stable across parameterizations ($\\varepsilon < 0.02$; Appendix~\\ref{sec:asv_stability}).\n",
        "numLines": 119,
        "startLine": 1,
        "totalLines": 119
      }
    },

### Tool result

_source line 3305 · 2026-05-24T18:26:56.569Z_

<details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_017BUezsusoYjnTpbxREam1v`</summary>

```text
1	\begin{abstract}
2	When LLM-generated annotations feed into causal inference, annotation quality is the wrong thing to worry about; the causal DAG topology determines whether errors attenuate, amplify, or reverse the treatment effect estimate. We derive bias expressions for seven canonical topologies under $K$-class non-differential misclassification in linear structural equation models and show that $K {\geq} 3$ introduces a structural possibility of sign reversal absent in the binary case. Under proportional effects, five topologies preserve the treatment effect sign under regularity conditions; exposure and IV can reverse it. Under empirical LLM confusion matrices, sign reversal is confined to exposure topology. We introduce Annotation Sensitivity Values (ASVs), with proved ($K{=}2$, $K{=}3$ symmetric) and extensively validated (general $K{\geq}3$) monotonicity that yields a vulnerability ranking from IV (maximally fragile) to M-bias (immune), and show that matrix-based corrections are provably ineffective under linear regression for four topologies due to Frisch--Waugh--Lovell invariance. A case study with 10 real LLM confusion matrices confirms: topology-aware correction reduces exposure bias from 61\% to $<$1\%, while topology-blind corrections are provably futile under linear regression for four topologies.
3	\end{abstract}
4	
```

</details>

```text
1	\section{Related Work}
2	\label{sec:related_work}
3	
4	\paragraph{Measurement error in causal inference.}
5	Foundations appear in \citet{carroll2006, gustafson2004, buonaccorsi2010, bound2001measurement}, with DAG-based formalization by \citet{hernan_cole2009} and \citet{kuroki_pearl2014}. For binary variables, non-differential misclassification biases toward the null~\cite{bross1954misclassification, brenner1993, ogburn_vanderweele2012}, but this fails for polytomous exposures~\cite{dosemeci1990, jurek2005, veirod_laake2001}. Extensions address differential error~\cite{edwards_lessler_naimi2021, imai_yamamoto2010}, mismeasured covariates~\cite{shu_yi2019a, shu_yi2019b}, mediation~\cite{valeri_vanderweele2013}, and nonclassical error~\cite{schennach2020mismeasured, nguimkeu_denteh_tchernis2019, wei2025multiply}. All analyze a single DAG; ours characterizes how the same confusion matrix produces qualitatively different bias across seven topologies.
6	
7	\paragraph{Text classifiers and LLM annotation.}
8	\citet{grimmer2022} and \citet{feder_etal2022} provide foundations for text-as-data causal inference. \citet{wood_doughty2018} first applied DAG reasoning to classifier error; \citet{teblunthuis2024} gave the most complete binary treatment ($K{=}2$, four DAGs); \citet{lee_wood_doughty2024} extended to categorical LLM classifiers but covered only confounding. On annotation quality, \citet{gilardi2023chatgpt} and \citet{ziems2024can} document rapid LLM adoption~\cite{tan2024llm}, \citet{baumann2025} found 31\% incorrect conclusions, and \citet{kim_garg2025} showed 60\% error correlation~\cite{pangakis_wolken_fasching2023, cheng_mayya_sedoc2024, audinet2025, keith_jensen_oconnor2020, variance_aware_llm2026, hidden_measurement_error2026}. These works either restrict to binary labels, cover a single topology, or diagnose quality without connecting it to causal validity.
9	
10	\paragraph{Sensitivity analysis and correction methods.}
11	Our ASV framework draws on E-values~\cite{vanderweele_ding2017, mathur2018}, robustness values~\cite{cinelli2020making}, and mediation sensitivity~\cite{imai_keele_yamamoto2010, ding_vanderweele2016, pryzant_etal2021}, but parametrizes annotation error severity rather than omitted variable strength. \citet{lash2009}, \citet{blackwell_honaker_king2017}, \citet{mahajan2006}, and \citet{hu2008} provide complementary frameworks for misclassified regressors. For correction, PPI++~\cite{angelopoulos2023} and DSL~\cite{egami_hinck_stewart_wei2023} are topology-agnostic~\cite{zrnic_candes2024, demirel2024prediction, cadei2025, gligoric2025}. We show this agnosticism is costly: correction effectiveness is topology-dependent, with matrix-based methods provably ineffective under FWL decomposition for four topologies.
12	
```

```text
1	\section{Proofs of Proposition~\ref{prop:signflip} and Theorem~\ref{thm:monotone}}
2	\label{app:proof}
3	
4	We provide algebraic proofs of sign preservation for the confounding and collider topologies (Proposition~\ref{prop:signflip}(a)--(b)), an explicit counterexample for exposure (Proposition~\ref{prop:signflip}(c)), numerical verification for mediation, M-bias, and front-door (Numerical Verification~1), and the monotonicity proof for confounding (Theorem~\ref{thm:monotone}).
5	Throughout, $A\in\{0,1,\dots,K{-}1\}$ with prior $p_k=P(A{=}k)$, and $C$ is a $K{\times}K$ column-stochastic confusion matrix with $C_{jk}=P(A^*{=}j\mid A{=}k)$.
6	We use category~0 as the reference and let $D_j=\mathbf{1}(A{=}j)$, $D_j^*=\mathbf{1}(A^*{=}j)$ for $j=1,\dots,K{-}1$.
7	
8	\subsection{Notation and Key Identities}
9	\label{app:notation}
10	
11	Under non-differential misclassification, the misclassified moments factor linearly through $C$.
12	Define the population vectors:
13	\begin{align}
14	  q_j &= P(A^*{=}j) = \textstyle\sum_k C_{jk}\,p_k, \label{eq:q}\\
15	  \mathrm{ct}_j &= \mathbb{E}[T\cdot D_j^*] = \textstyle\sum_k C_{jk}\,\mathbb{E}[T\cdot D_k], \label{eq:ct}\\
16	  \mathrm{cy}_j &= \mathbb{E}[Y\cdot D_j^*] = \textstyle\sum_k C_{jk}\,\mathbb{E}[Y\cdot D_k]. \label{eq:cy}
17	\end{align}
18	Write $q = Cp$, $\mathrm{ct} = C\,\mathbb{E}[T\cdot D]$, and $\mathrm{cy} = C\,\mathbb{E}[Y\cdot D]$, where $D=(D_0,\dots,D_{K-1})^\top$.
19	
20	\subsection{Frisch--Waugh--Lovell Decomposition}
21	\label{app:fwl}
22	
23	For topologies where $T$ is directly observed (confounding, mediation, collider, M-bias, front-door), the estimator regresses $Y$ on $Z^*=(1,T,D_1^*,\dots,D_{K-1}^*)$.
24	By the Frisch--Waugh--Lovell (FWL) theorem, the coefficient on $T$ satisfies:
25	\begin{equation}
26	  \operatorname{plim}\hat{\tau}(C)
27	  = \frac{\mathbb{E}[\tilde{T}\,\tilde{Y}]}{\mathbb{E}[\tilde{T}^2]},
28	  \label{eq:fwl}
29	\end{equation}
30	where $\tilde{T}=T-\mathbb{E}[T\mid A^*]$ and $\tilde{Y}=Y-\mathbb{E}[Y\mid A^*]$ are residuals from projecting onto $(1,D_1^*,\dots,D_{K-1}^*)$.
31	
32	\begin{lemma}[Bias decomposition]
33	\label{lem:bias_decomp}
34	Suppose $Y = \tau T + f(A) + \varepsilon_Y$ with $\varepsilon_Y \perp (T, A, A^*)$.
35	Then
36	\begin{equation}
37	  \operatorname{plim}\hat\tau(C) = \tau + \frac{R(C)}{D(C)},
38	  \label{eq:bias_decomp}
39	\end{equation}
40	where
41	\begin{align}
42	  R(C) &= \mathbb{E}\bigl[\operatorname{Cov}(T,\,f(A)\mid A^*)\bigr], \label{eq:R}\\
43	  D(C) &= \mathbb{E}\bigl[\operatorname{Var}(T\mid A^*)\bigr] > 0. \label{eq:D}
44	\end{align}
45	\end{lemma}
46	
47	\begin{proof}
48	Expand $\tilde{Y}$ using $Y=\tau T + f(A) + \varepsilon_Y$:
49	\[
50	  \tilde{Y} = \tau\,\tilde{T} + \bigl(f(A) - \mathbb{E}[f(A)\mid A^*]\bigr) + \varepsilon_Y.
51	\]
52	Since $\tilde{T}$ is measurable w.r.t.\ $(T,A^*)$ and $\varepsilon_Y\perp(T,A,A^*)$:
53	\[
54	  \mathbb{E}[\tilde{T}\,\tilde{Y}]
55	  = \tau\,\mathbb{E}[\tilde{T}^2]
56	  + \mathbb{E}\bigl[\tilde{T}\,\bigl(f(A)-\mathbb{E}[f(A)\mid A^*]\bigr)\bigr].
57	\]
58	The numerator residual is $R(C) = \mathbb{E}[\tilde{T}(f(A)-\mathbb{E}[f(A)\mid A^*])]$, and the denominator is $D(C) = \mathbb{E}[\tilde{T}^2]$.
59	
60	By the law of total covariance:
61	\begin{align*}
62	  R(C) &= \sum_{j=0}^{K-1} q_j\,\operatorname{Cov}(T,\,f(A)\mid A^*{=}j)\\
63	  &= \mathbb{E}[\operatorname{Cov}(T,f(A)\mid A^*)].
64	\end{align*}
65	For $D(C)>0$: decompose $T = g(A) + \varepsilon_T$ where $g(A)=\mathbb{E}[T\mid A]$ (for confounding) or simply note that $T$ has exogenous variation independent of $A^*$ (for mediation).
66	In both cases, $\tilde{T}=\bigl(g(A)-\mathbb{E}[g(A)\mid A^*]\bigr)+\varepsilon_T'$, where $\varepsilon_T'$ is the component of $T$ independent of $(A,A^*)$.
67	Hence $D(C)\geq\operatorname{Var}(\varepsilon_T')>0$.
68	\end{proof}
69	
70	\subsection{Confounding Topology ($A$ = Confounder)}
71	\label{app:confounding}
72	
73	\paragraph{Data-generating process.}
74	$A\to T$, $A\to Y$, $T\to Y$.
75	Using dummy coding with category 0 as reference:
76	\begin{align*}
77	  T &= \textstyle\sum_{j=1}^{K-1}\alpha_j D_j + \varepsilon_T,\quad
78	  \varepsilon_T\sim\mathcal{N}(0,\sigma_T^2),\\
79	  Y &= \tau\, T + \textstyle\sum_{j=1}^{K-1}\beta_j^{(A)} D_j + \varepsilon_Y,\\
80	  &\quad \varepsilon_Y\sim\mathcal{N}(0,1),
81	\end{align*}
82	with $\varepsilon_T\perp A$ and $\varepsilon_Y\perp(T,A)$.
83	Define $g(k)=\sum_{j}\alpha_j\mathbf{1}(k{=}j)$ and
84	$f(k)=\sum_{j}\beta_j^{(A)}\mathbf{1}(k{=}j)$, so $g(0)=f(0)=0$.
85	
86	\paragraph{Closed-form plim.}
87	Using the FWL decomposition, the coefficient on $T$ is:
88	\begin{equation}
89	  \operatorname{plim}\hat\tau(C)
90	  = \frac{\mu_{TY} - \sum_{j=0}^{K-1}\frac{\mathrm{ct}_j\,\mathrm{cy}_j}{q_j}}
91	  {s_T - \sum_{j=0}^{K-1}\frac{\mathrm{ct}_j^2}{q_j}},
92	  \label{eq:plim_conf}
93	\end{equation}
94	where $s_T=\mathbb{E}[T^2]$, $\mu_{TY}=\mathbb{E}[TY]$, and $\mathrm{ct}_j$, $\mathrm{cy}_j$, $q_j$ are defined in~\eqref{eq:q}--\eqref{eq:cy}.
95	
96	\begin{proof}[Derivation]
97	By FWL, $\operatorname{plim}\hat\tau = \mathbb{E}[\tilde{T}Y]/\mathbb{E}[\tilde{T}^2]$ where $\tilde{T}=T-\mathbb{E}[T\mid A^*]$.
98	Since $\mathbb{E}[T\mid A^*{=}j]=\mathrm{ct}_j/q_j$:
99	\begin{align*}
100	  \mathbb{E}[\tilde{T}^2]

$D(C)\\geq\\operatorname{Var}(\\varepsilon_T')>0$.\n\\end{proof}\n\n\\subsection{Confounding Topology ($A$ = Confounder)}\n\\label{app:confounding}\n\n\\paragraph{Data-generating process.}\n$A\\to T$, $A\\to Y$, $T\\to Y$.\nUsing dummy coding with category 0 as reference:\n\\begin{align*}\n  T &= \\textstyle\\sum_{j=1}^{K-1}\\alpha_j D_j + \\varepsilon_T,\\quad\n \\varepsilon_Y\\sim\\mathcal{N}(0,1),\n\\end{align*}\nwith $\\varepsilon_T\\perp A$ and $\\varepsilon_Y\\perp(T,A)$.\nDefine $g(k)=\\sum_{j}\\alpha_j\\mathbf{1}(k{=}j)$ and\n$f(k)=\\sum_{j}\\beta_j^{(A)}\\mathbf{1}(k{=}j)$, so $g(0)=f(0)=0$.\n\n\\paragraph{Closed-form plim.}\nUsing the FWL decomposition, the coefficient on $T$ is:\n\\begin{equation}\n in~\\eqref{eq:q}--\\eqref{eq:cy}.\n\n\\begin{proof}[Derivation]\nBy FWL, $\\operatorname{plim}\\hat\\tau = \\mathbb{E}[\\tilde{T}Y]/\\mathbb{E}[\\tilde{T}^2]$ where $\\tilde{T}=T-\\mathbb{E}[T\\mid 4:We validate through Monte Carlo simulation ($K{=}3$), cross-$K$ robustness checks ($K{=}5,7$), and empirical validation with real LLM confusion matrices from three NLP tasks ($K{=}2,3,10$).
9:For each topology, we sample 100{,}000 confusion matrices from a Dirichlet$(1,1,1)$ prior, compute analytical probability limits, and validate against Monte Carlo simulation ($N{=}10{,}000$). ASV experiments use $C(\delta) = (1{-}\delta)I + \delta C_0$.
15:Analytical probability limits match Monte Carlo estimates with MAPE of 1.6\%, averaged across 161 scenarios (23 LLM configurations $\times$ 7 topologies; per-topology range: 0.3\%--3.7\%).

12	\begin{enumerate}
13	\item \textbf{Multi-class bias taxonomy.} We derive bias expressions for seven canonical DAG topologies\footnote{These span the four structural roles of the annotated variable: treatment (exposure), outcome-side covariate (confounding, mediation, M-bias, front-door), selection variable (collider), and instrument (IV).} (Figure~\ref{fig:dag_taxonomy}) under $K$-class non-differential misclassification and establish a sign-preservation/reversal dichotomy: five topologies preserve the treatment effect sign, while exposure and IV can reverse it.
14	
15	\item \textbf{Annotation Sensitivity Values.} We define three ASV diagnostics (magnitude, significance, and sign-flip) with proved envelope monotonicity (Theorems~\ref{thm:monotone},~\ref{thm:k3_symmetric}) and $\varepsilon$-approximate monotonicity for general $K{\geq}3$ (Conjecture~\ref{conj:monotone}), revealing a vulnerability spectrum from IV (maximally fragile) to M-bias (immune).
16	
17	\item \textbf{Topology-aware correction routing.} FWL invariance renders matrix-based corrections provably ineffective for four covariate topologies. We evaluate five correction methods and provide a topology-aware decision procedure (Algorithm~\ref{alg:correction}, Appendix), validated with real LLM confusion matrices.
18	\end{enumerate}
```

<details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_01G14JSYGK74J6KX4Li73JUM`</summary>

```text
79	The ASV envelope is $\varepsilon$-approximately monotonic for non-IV topologies ($\varepsilon < 0.02$ for non-exposure under moderate effects, larger for exposure; Conjecture~\ref{conj:monotone}). Figure~\ref{fig:asv_ranking} shows the vulnerability ranking: IV is maximally fragile, M-bias is immune ($\text{ASV} = \infty$). Sign-flip ASV: 83.4\% of exposure matrices yield $\delta^*_{\text{sf}} < 1$; IV ranges 1.1--6.7 (Table~\ref{tab:asv_summary}). Significance ASV exhibits a phase transition at $N^* \approx 6{,}000$--$10{,}000$; bootstrap CIs ($B{=}1{,}000$; Appendix~\ref{sec:bootstrap_asv}) confirm stable topology ranking despite wide IV intervals.
```

/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-prod_0510/projects/causal_annot_error_attrib/docs/paper/experiments.tex:100:We evaluate five correction methods (naive plug-in, MLA, DSL~\cite{egami_hinck_stewart_wei2023}, MC-SIMEX~\cite{kuchenhoff2006simex}, PPI++~\cite{angelopoulos2023}) across all topologies (210 configurations; Table~\ref{tab:correction}, Appendix~\ref{app:supplementary}). Regression calibration~\cite{carroll2006} is designed for continuous measurement error and does not apply to categorical misclassification.
/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-prod_0510/projects/causal_annot_error_attrib/docs/paper/experiments.tex:106:PPI++ achieves the highest RMSE reduction (47.0\% at $K{=}3$) but requires gold labels and harms M-bias ($-$88\%); MC-SIMEX is gold-free and $K$-robust (27--70\%), though it harms front-door ($-$62.4\%); rankings are stable across $K$ ($\rho \geq 0.80$; Appendix~\ref{sec:correction_k_sensitivity}).
/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-prod_0510/projects/causal_annot_error_attrib/docs/paper/experiments.tex:109:Algorithm~\ref{alg:correction} (Appendix) routes through three stages: risk assessment (exposure/IV high; M-bias low), method selection (PPI++ with gold, MC-SIMEX/DSL without), and FWL screening (skip plug-in/MLA for covariate topologies). Under topology uncertainty, ASV under candidate topologies provides worst-case sensitivity.
/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-prod_0510/projects/causal_annot_error_attrib/docs/paper/experiments.tex:112:ClinicalBERT and HumAID ($K{=}10$) confirm all patterns under linear and logistic models ($|\Delta| < 0.02$; Appendix~\ref{app:case_study},~\ref{sec:correction_k_sensitivity}). End-to-end demos (Table~\ref{tab:e2e-demo}; Appendix~\ref{sec:real_cm_demo}) validate: PPI++ reduces exposure bias from 42.9\% to 0.2\%, confounding achieves 0\% improvement (FWL), and topology-agnostic IV correction amplifies bias ($-$139\%).
/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-prod_0510/projects/causal_annot_error_attrib/docs/paper/conclusion.tex:4:We characterized how multi-class annotation errors interact with causal DAG topology: at $K{\geq}3$, exposure and IV can reverse treatment effect signs while five topologies preserve them. ASVs provide a topology-dependent vulnerability ranking; matrix-based corrections are provably ineffective for four covariate topologies (FWL invariance). Practitioners should identify the topology first: for exposure, PPI++ reduces bias by 93.6\%; for IV, all tested corrections worsen the estimate.
/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-prod_0510/projects/causal_annot_error_attrib/docs/paper/method.tex:85:[Omitted long matching line]
/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-prod_0510/projects/causal_annot_error_attrib/docs/paper/e2e_demo_table.tex:12:Exposure & -42.87 $\pm$ 2.42 & 0.239 & PPI++ & -0.22 $\pm$ 4.94 & +90.9\% \\
/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-prod_0510/projects/causal_annot_error_attrib/docs/paper/e2e_demo_table.tex:16:\caption{End-to-end demo over 100 replicates (N=10,000, K=3, 20\% gold). Confounding: plug-in corrections leave the treatment coefficient unchanged (FWL invariance), so Algorithm~1 routes to ``no correction''. Exposure: PPI++ recovers the target coefficient. IV: Wald estimator instability produces large bias; PPI++ (OLS-based) is limited, routing to MC-SIMEX.}
/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-prod_0510/projects/causal_annot_error_attrib/docs/paper/appendix_proofs.tex:609:When $|\tau|>\bar{B}_{\mathrm{col}}$, the bias cannot overwhelm $\tau$. In practice, $\operatorname{Var}(g(A))$ and $\operatorname{Var}(h(A))$ can be estimated from observable data by bootstrapping regression coefficients from $A^*$-stratified subsamples, yielding conservative upper bounds on $\bar{B}_{\mathrm{col}}$.

    "originalFile": "\\begin{abstract}\nWhen LLM-generated annotations feed into causal inference, annotation quality is the wrong thing to worry about; the causal DAG topology determines whether errors attenuate, amplify, or reverse the treatment effect estimate. We derive bias expressions for seven canonical topologies under $K$-class non-differential misclassification in linear structural equation models and show that $K {\\geq} 3$ introduces a structural possibility of sign reversal absent in the binary case. Under proportional effects, five topologies preserve the treatment effect sign under regularity conditions; exposure and IV can reverse it. Under empirical LLM confusion matrices, sign reversal is confined to exposure topology. We introduce Annotation Sensitivity Values (ASVs), with proved ($K{=}2$, $K{=}3$ symmetric) and extensively validated (general $K{\\geq}3$) monotonicity that yields a vulnerability ranking from IV (maximally fragile) to M-bias (immune), and show that matrix-based corrections are provably ineffective under linear regression for four topologies due to Frisch--Waugh--Lovell invariance. A case study with 10 real LLM confusion matrices confirms: topology-aware correction reduces exposure bias from 61\\% to $<$1\\%, while topology-blind corrections are provably futile under linear regression for four topologies.\n\\end{abstract}\n",
    "structuredPatch": [

          " \\begin{abstract}",
          "-When LLM-generated annotations feed into causal inference, annotation quality is the wrong thing to worry about; the causal DAG topology determines whether errors attenuate, amplify, or reverse the treatment effect estimate. We derive bias expressions for seven canonical topologies under $K$-class non-differential misclassification in linear structural equation models and show that $K {\\geq} 3$ introduces a structural possibility of sign reversal absent in the binary case. Under proportional effects, five topologies preserve the treatment effect sign under regularity conditions; exposure and IV can reverse it. Under empirical LLM confusion matrices, sign reversal is confined to exposure topology. We introduce Annotation Sensitivity Values (ASVs), with proved ($K{=}2$, $K{=}3$ symmetric) and extensively validated (general $K{\\geq}3$) monotonicity that yields a vulnerability ranking from IV (maximally fragile) to M-bias (immune), and show that matrix-based corrections are provably ineffective under linear regression for four topologies due to Frisch--Waugh--Lovell invariance. A case study with 10 real LLM confusion matrices confirms: topology-aware correction reduces exposure bias from 61\\% to $<$1\\%, while topology-blind corrections are provably futile under linear regression for four topologies.",
          "+When large language model (LLM)-generated annotations feed into causal inference, annotation quality is the wrong thing to worry about; the causal directed acyclic graph (DAG) topology determines whether errors attenuate, amplify, or reverse the treatment effect estimate. We derive bias expressions for seven canonical topologies under $K$-class non-differential misclassification in linear structural equation models and show that $K {\\geq} 3$ introduces a structural possibility of sign reversal absent in the binary case. Under proportional effects, five topologies preserve the treatment effect sign under regularity conditions; exposure and instrumental variable (IV) can reverse it. Under empirical LLM confusion matrices, sign reversal is confined to exposure topology. We introduce Annotation Sensitivity Values (ASVs), with proved ($K{=}2$, $K{=}3$ symmetric) and extensively validated (general $K{\\geq}3$) monotonicity that yields a vulnerability ranking from IV (maximally fragile) to M-bias (immune), and show that matrix-based corrections are provably ineffective under linear regression for four topologies due to Frisch--Waugh--Lovell invariance. A case study with 10 real LLM confusion matrices confirms: topology-aware correction reduces exposure bias from 61\\% to $<$1\\%, while topology-blind corrections are provably futile under linear regression for four topologies.",
          " \\end{abstract}"
        ]

Most tasks are inherently multi-class (stance detection: 3--4 categories, sentiment: 5, disaster response: 10+), and annotation errors propagate into downstream causal estimates in ways that depend on both error structure and causal design. \\citet{baumann2025} show that 31\\% of hypotheses yield incorrect conclusions with state-of-the-art LLMs, with 60\\% error correlation across models~\\cite{kim_garg2025}. <details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_0137ADe72SAre9zEaJTs7w9k`</summary>

```text
3	
4	Large language models (LLMs) are rapidly replacing human annotators in computational social science~\cite{gilardi2023chatgpt, ziems2024can, egami_hinck_stewart_wei2023, baumann2025}. Most tasks are inherently multi-class (stance detection: 3--4 categories, sentiment: 5, disaster response: 10+), and annotation errors propagate into downstream causal estimates in ways that depend on both error structure and causal design. \citet{baumann2025} show that 31\% of hypotheses yield incorrect conclusions with state-of-the-art LLMs, with 60\% error correlation across models~\cite{kim_garg2025}. Standard metrics do not capture this risk: within $F_1$ 0.61--0.69, causal bias varies by an order of magnitude across topologies.
5	
6	The transition at $K{\geq}3$ is not gradual but structural. For $K{=}2$, the stochastic constraint forces any confusion matrix with positive diagonal to preserve coefficient signs, an algebraic property of the $2{\times}2$ matrix dimension~\cite{brenner1993}. This guarantee vanishes at $K{\geq}3$: the higher-dimensional confusion matrix admits negative effective weights on categories with opposite-sign coefficients; sign reversal can occur even under high accuracy~\cite{veirod_laake2001}. Under a uniform Dirichlet prior, 83.4\% of $K{=}3$ exposure confusion matrices produce sign reversal of at least one secondary coefficient; under realistic accuracy (diagonal $\geq 0.7$), 2.1\% still do. The primary treatment coefficient is never reversed under the tested $\beta$ configurations where a single category dominates ($\beta_1 \gg \beta_2$), an empirical regularity we discuss in Section~\ref{sec:method}.
7	
8	$F_1$ does not exhibit significant rank correlation with causal bias ($\rho = -0.10$, $p = 0.45$ across 56 HumAID scenarios; bias 4.1--42.1\% within $F_1$ 0.614--0.690). Prior work noted polytomous sign reversal~\cite{dosemeci1990, veirod_laake2001} and confounder amplification~\cite{brenner1993}, but covered at most one topology~\cite{lee_wood_doughty2024}. We present the first systematic analysis jointly addressing multi-class ($K{\geq}3$) misclassification across seven DAG topologies, with analytical predictions matching Monte Carlo within 1.6\% MAPE across 161 scenarios.
```

    "originalFile": "\\section{Introduction}\n\\label{sec:introduction}\n\nLarge language models (LLMs) are rapidly replacing human annotators in computational social science~\\cite{gilardi2023chatgpt, ziems2024can, egami_hinck_stewart_wei2023, baumann2025}. Most tasks are inherently multi-class (stance detection: 3--4 categories, sentiment: 5, disaster response: 10+), and annotation errors propagate into downstream causal estimates in ways that depend on both error structure and causal design. \\citet{baumann2025} show that 31\\% of hypotheses yield incorrect conclusions with state-of-the-art LLMs, with 60\\% error correlation across models~\\cite{kim_garg2025}. Standard metrics do not capture this risk: within $F_1$ 0.61--0.69, causal bias varies by an order of magnitude across topologies.\n\nThe transition at $K{\\geq}3$ is not gradual but structural. For $K{=}2$, the stochastic constraint forces any confusion matrix with positive diagonal to preserve coefficient signs, an algebraic property of the $2{\\times}2$ matrix dimension~\\cite{brenner1993}. This guarantee vanishes at $K{\\geq}3$: the higher-dimensional confusion matrix admits negative effective weights on categories with opposite-sign coefficients; sign reversal can occur even under high accuracy~\\cite{veirod_laake2001}. Under a uniform Dirichlet prior, 83.4\\% of $K{=}3$ exposure confusion matrices produce sign reversal of at least one secondary coefficient; under realistic accuracy (diagonal $\\geq 0.7$), 2.1\\% still do. The primary treatment coefficient is never reversed under the tested $\\beta$ configurations where a single category dominates ($\\beta_1 \\gg \\beta_2$), an empirical regularity we discuss in Section~\\ref{sec:method}.\n\n$F_1$ does not exhibit significant rank correlation with causal bias ($\\rho = -0.10$, $p = 0.45$ across 56 HumAID scenarios; bias 4.1--42.1\\% within $F_1$ 0.614--0.690). Prior work noted polytomous sign reversal~\\cite{dosemeci1990, veirod_laake2001} and confounder amplification~\\cite{brenner1993}, but covered at most one topology~\\cite{lee_wood_doughty2024}. We present the first systematic analysis jointly addressing multi-class ($K{\\geq}3$) misclassification across seven DAG topologies, with analytical predictions matching Monte Carlo within 1.6\\% MAPE across 161 scenarios.\n\nWe address these gaps with three contributions:\n\n\\begin{enumerate}\n\\item \\textbf{Multi-class bias taxonomy.} We derive bias expressions for seven canonical DAG topologies\\footnote{These span the four structural roles of the annotated variable: treatment (exposure), outcome-side covariate (confounding, mediation, M-bias, front-door), selection variable (collider), and instrument (IV).} (Figure~\\ref{fig:dag_taxonomy}) under $K$-class non-differential misclassification and establish a sign-preservation/reversal dichotomy: five topologies preserve the treatment effect sign, while exposure and IV can reverse it.\n\n\\item \\textbf{Annotation Sensitivity Values.} We define three ASV diagnostics (magnitude, significance, and sign-flip) with proved envelope monotonicity (Theorems~\\ref{thm:monotone},~\\ref{thm:k3_symmetric}) and $\\varepsilon$-approximate monotonicity for general $K{\\geq}3$ (Conjecture~\\ref{conj:monotone}), revealing a vulnerability spectrum from IV (maximally fragile) to M-bias (immune).\n\n\\item \\textbf{Topology-aware correction routing.} FWL invariance renders matrix-based corrections provably ineffective for four covariate topologies. We evaluate five correction methods and provide a topology-aware decision procedure (Algorithm~\\ref{alg:correction}, Appendix), validated with real LLM confusion matrices.\n\\end{enumerate}\n",

Let $\\tau$ denote the target causal parameter, whose precise definition is topology-specific (e.g., the coefficient on $X$ for confounding, the category-specific $\\beta_j$ for exposure, or the direct effect $\\tau_{\\text{direct}}$ for mediation), and $\\hat{\\tau}(A^*)$ the estimator using misclassified annotations.",
