    "originalFile": "\\section{Introduction}\n\\label{sec:introduction}\n\nLarge language models (LLMs) are rapidly replacing human annotators in computational social science~\\cite{gilardi2023chatgpt, ziems2024can, egami_hinck_stewart_wei2023, baumann2025}. Most tasks are inherently multi-class (stance detection: 3--4 categories, sentiment: 5, disaster response: 10+), and annotation errors propagate into downstream causal estimates in ways that depend on both error structure and causal design. \\citet{baumann2025} show that 31\\% of hypotheses yield incorrect conclusions with state-of-the-art LLMs, with 60\\% error correlation across models~\\cite{kim_garg2025}. Standard metrics do not capture this risk: within $F_1$ 0.61--0.69, causal bias varies by an order of magnitude across topologies.\n\nThe transition at $K{\\geq}3$ is not gradual but structural. For $K{=}2$, the stochastic constraint forces any confusion matrix with positive diagonal to preserve coefficient signs, an algebraic property of the $2{\\times}2$ matrix dimension~\\cite{brenner1993}. This guarantee vanishes at $K{\\geq}3$: the higher-dimensional confusion matrix admits negative effective weights on categories with opposite-sign coefficients; sign reversal can occur even under high accuracy~\\cite{veirod_laake2001}. Under a uniform Dirichlet prior, 83.4\\% of $K{=}3$ exposure confusion matrices produce sign reversal of at least one secondary coefficient; under realistic accuracy (diagonal $\\geq 0.7$), 2.1\\% still do. The primary treatment coefficient is never reversed under the tested $\\beta$ configurations where a single category dominates ($\\beta_1 \\gg \\beta_2$), an empirical regularity we discuss in Section~\\ref{sec:method}.\n\n$F_1$ does not exhibit significant rank correlation with causal bias ($\\rho = -0.10$, $p = 0.45$ across 56 HumAID scenarios; bias 4.1--42.1\\% within $F_1$ 0.614--0.690). Prior work noted polytomous sign reversal~\\cite{dosemeci1990, veirod_laake2001} and confounder amplification~\\cite{brenner1993}, but covered at most one topology~\\cite{lee_wood_doughty2024}. We present the first systematic analysis jointly addressing multi-class ($K{\\geq}3$) misclassification across seven directed acyclic graph (DAG) topologies, with analytical predictions matching Monte Carlo within 1.6\\% MAPE across 161 scenarios.\n\nWe address these gaps with three contributions:\n\n\\begin{enumerate}\n\\item \\textbf{Multi-class bias taxonomy.} We derive bias expressions for seven canonical DAG topologies\\footnote{These span the four structural roles of the annotated variable: treatment (exposure), outcome-side covariate (confounding, mediation, M-bias, front-door), selection variable (collider), and instrument (IV).} (Figure~\\ref{fig:dag_taxonomy}) under $K$-class non-differential misclassification and establish a sign-preservation/reversal dichotomy: five topologies preserve the treatment effect sign, while exposure and IV can reverse it.\n\n\\item \\textbf{Annotation Sensitivity Values.} We define three ASV diagnostics (magnitude, significance, and sign-flip) with proved envelope monotonicity (Theorems~\\ref{thm:monotone},~\\ref{thm:k3_symmetric}) and $\\varepsilon$-approximate monotonicity for general $K{\\geq}3$ (Conjecture~\\ref{conj:monotone}), revealing a vulnerability spectrum from IV (maximally fragile) to M-bias (immune).\n\n\\item \\textbf{Topology-aware correction routing.} Frisch--Waugh--Lovell (FWL) invariance renders matrix-based corrections provably ineffective for four covariate topologies. We evaluate five correction methods and provide a topology-aware decision procedure (Algorithm~\\ref{alg:correction}, Appendix), validated with real LLM confusion matrices.\n\\end{enumerate}\n",

    "originalFile": "\\section{Experiments}\n\\label{sec:experiments}\n\nWe validate through Monte Carlo simulation ($K{=}3$), cross-$K$ robustness checks ($K{=}5,7$), and empirical validation with real LLM confusion matrices from three NLP tasks ($K{=}2,3,10$).\n\n\\subsection{Setup}\n\\label{sec:setup}\n\nFor each topology, we sample 100{,}000 confusion matrices from a Dirichlet$(1,1,1)$ prior, compute analytical probability limits, and validate against Monte Carlo simulation ($N{=}10{,}000$). ASV experiments use $C(\\delta) = (1{-}\\delta)I + \\delta C_0$.\n\n\\subsection{Bias Taxonomy}\n\\label{sec:taxonomy_results}\n\n\\paragraph{Theory-MC agreement.}\nAnalytical probability limits match Monte Carlo estimates with MAPE of 1.6\\%, averaged across 161 scenarios (23 LLM configurations $\\times$ 7 topologies; per-topology range: 0.3\\%--3.7\\%).\n\n\\paragraph{Exposure DAG: sign reversal under multi-class misclassification.}\nUnder realistic accuracy (diagonal $\\geq 0.7$), the violation rate is 13.7\\% with 2.1\\% sign reversal; at diagonal $\\geq 0.9$, violation persists at 11.9\\% (Appendix~\\ref{sec:iv_dirichlet_subspace}). Under the full Dirichlet prior, 85\\% of matrices violate the binary ``toward-null'' guarantee and 83.4\\% cause sign reversal; $\\min_k C_{kk} < 0.55$ predicts sign reversal with 91\\% accuracy (Appendix~\\ref{sec:sign_determinants}). The $K{=}2$ control confirms classical attenuation~\\cite{brenner1993}, establishing the multi-class failure as structurally distinct.\n\n\\paragraph{Structural guarantee for non-exposure topologies.}\nFive topologies exhibit zero sign-flip rate (Table~\\ref{tab:taxonomy}): confounding, mediation, and front-door show 100\\% amplification~\\cite{brenner1993, dosemeci1990} but never reverse the sign; collider is protective ($|B(C)|$ non-increasing; Conjecture~\\ref{conj:monotone}); M-bias shows weakest sensitivity (53.2\\% violation). All patterns persist at $K{=}5,7$ (Appendix~\\ref{sec:cross_k_robustness}).\n\n\\paragraph{IV: Wald estimator instability.}\nThe IV topology is the sole non-exposure exception: 52.6\\% of random matrices cause sign reversal, stable across $K{=}3,5,7$. Diagonal dominance ($C_{kk} > 0.5$) eliminates sign reversal but preserves amplification (Appendix~\\ref{sec:iv_dirichlet_subspace}); however, all 23 empirical LLM configurations show zero IV sign reversal.\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\caption{Bias under $K{=}3$ non-differential misclassification (100{,}000 Dirichlet$(1,1,1)$ matrices). Violation: $|\\text{bias}| > 0$; Sign Flip: any coefficient reverses sign; Amplif.: $|\\hat{\\tau}| > |\\tau|$. $\\tau_{\\text{sf}}{=}0$?: sign-flip guarantee holds unconditionally (Yes), conditionally (Cond.; Proposition~\\ref{prop:signflip}), or not (No). Collider 100\\% amplification reflects Berkson bias; misclassification is protective.}\n\\label{tab:taxonomy}\n\\small\n\\resizebox{\\columnwidth}{!}{\\begin{tabular}{lcccc}\n\\toprule\n\\textbf{Topology} & \\textbf{Violation} & \\textbf{Sign Flip} & \\textbf{Amplif.} & $\\tau_{\\text{sf}}{=}0$? \\\\\n\\midrule\nConfounding & 100\\% & 0\\% & 100\\% & Cond. \\\\\nExposure & 85\\% & 83.4\\% & 14.1\\% & No \\\\\nMediation & 100\\% & 0\\% & 100\\% & Cond. \\\\\nFront-door & 100\\% & 0\\% & 100\\% & Cond. \\\\\nCollider & 100\\% & 0\\% & 100\\% & Cond. \\\\\nM-bias & 53.2\\% & 0\\% & 10.4\\% & Yes \\\\\nIV & 100\\% & 52.6\\% & 100\\% & No \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}}\n\\end{table}\n\nThe bias distributions are visualized in Figure~\\ref{fig:bias_distribution}.\n\n\\subsection{ASV Framework Validation}\n\\label{sec:asv_results}\n\n\\begin{figure*}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=0.85\\textwidth]{fig3_asv_ranking.pdf}\n\\caption{ASV vulnerability ranking across topologies. Three ASV types (magnitude, significance, sign-flip) reveal a topology-dependent spectrum from IV (maximally fragile) to M-bias (immune, ASV $= \\infty$).}\n\\label{fig:asv_ranking}\n\\end{figure*}\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\caption{ASV summary across topologies ($K{=}3$, $\\tau{=}0.5$). ---: not applicable.}\n\\label{tab:asv_summary}\n\\begin{tabular}{lccc}\n\\toprule\n\\textbf{Topology} & \\textbf{Mag ASV} & \\textbf{Power} & \\textbf{Sign-flip} \\\\\n\\midrule\nIV          & 0.10--0.61 & 0\\%   & 1.1--6.7 \\\\\nFront-door  & 0.06--0.39 & 100\\% & --- \\\\\nConfounding & 0.08--0.49 & 100\\% & --- \\\\\nMediation   & 0.08--0.45 & 0\\%   & --- \\\\\nCollider    & 0.34--2.03 & 0\\%   & --- \\\\\nM-bias      & $\\infty$   & 98\\%  & --- \\\\\nExposure    & ---        & ---   & 83.4\\% \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n\n\\paragraph{ASV results.}\nThe ASV envelope is $\\varepsilon$-approximately monotonic for non-IV topologies ($\\varepsilon < 0.02$ for non-exposure under moderate effects, larger for exposure; Conjecture~\\ref{conj:monotone}). Figure~\\ref{fig:asv_ranking} shows the vulnerability ranking: IV is maximally fragile, M-bias is immune ($\\text{ASV} = \\infty$). Sign-flip ASV: 83.4\\% of exposure matrices yield $\\delta^*_{\\text{sf}} < 1$; IV ranges 1.1--6.7 (Table~\\ref{tab:asv_summary}). Significance ASV exhibits a phase transition at $N^* \\approx 6{,}000$--$10{,}000$; bootstrap CIs ($B{=}1{,}000$; Appendix~\\ref{sec:bootstrap_asv}) confirm stable topology ranking despite wide IV intervals.\n\n\\subsection{Empirical Validation}\n\\label{sec:empirical}\n\nWe validate using real LLM confusion matrices from HumAID~\\cite{alam2021humaid} ($K{=}10$), VAST ($K{=}3$), and CivilComments ($K{=}2$): four LLMs, 23 configurations, 161 bias scenarios.\n\n\\paragraph{Empirical bias ranking.}\nHumAID ($K{=}10$) matches predictions: IV (12.4--42.1\\%) and exposure (9.0--17.4\\%) dominate; remaining topologies $<$15\\%. $F_1$ does not exhibit significant rank correlation with bias ($\\rho = -0.10$, $p = 0.45$; Figure~\\ref{fig:f1_vs_bias}), but per-topology patterns diverge ($\\rho \\leq -0.53$ for five topologies vs.\\ $0.87$ for M-bias; Appendix~\\ref{sec:within_topo_f1}). Of exposure coefficients, 14/23 (61\\%) exhibit sign reversal, all secondary; zero across 138 non-exposure scenarios.\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{fig5_f1_vs_bias.pdf}\n\\caption{Topology determines downstream causal bias, not $F_1$. HumAID $K{=}10$ (56 points): within $F_1$ range 0.614--0.690, bias varies $10{\\times}$ (IV 42.1\\% vs.\\ collider $<$5.6\\%).}\n\\label{fig:f1_vs_bias}\n\\end{figure}\n\n\n\\subsection{Correction Guidance}\n\\label{sec:correction}\n\nWe evaluate five correction methods (naive plug-in, MLA, DSL~\\cite{egami_hinck_stewart_wei2023}, MC-SIMEX~\\cite{kuchenhoff2006simex}, PPI++~\\cite{angelopoulos2023}) across all topologies (210 configurations; Table~\\ref{tab:correction}, Appendix~\\ref{app:supplementary}). Regression calibration~\\cite{carroll2006} is designed for continuous measurement error and does not apply to categorical misclassification.\n\n\\paragraph{FWL invariance.}\nUnder linear regression, FWL invariance renders matrix-based methods (naive plug-in, MLA) provably ineffective for covariate topologies, achieving 0\\% bias reduction (Appendix~\\ref{app:fwl_proof}); under logistic regression this breaks but ranking persists ($|\\Delta| < 0.02$; Appendix~\\ref{sec:fwl_nonlinear}). To our knowledge, this futility result (that matrix-based corrections cannot reduce covariate-induced bias under FWL decomposition) has not been explicitly stated in the measurement error literature~\\cite{carroll2006, buonaccorsi2010}.\n\n\\paragraph{Method ranking.}\nPPI++ achieves the highest RMSE reduction (47.0\\% at $K{=}3$) but requires gold labels and harms M-bias ($-$88\\%); MC-SIMEX is gold-free and $K$-robust (27--70\\%), though it harms front-door ($-$62.4\\%); rankings are stable across $K$ ($\\rho \\geq 0.80$; Appendix~\\ref{sec:correction_k_sensitivity}).\n\n\\paragraph{Correction decision procedure.}\nAlgorithm~\\ref{alg:correction} (Appendix) routes through three stages: risk assessment (exposure/IV high; M-bias low), method selection (PPI++ with gold, MC-SIMEX/DSL without), and FWL screening (skip plug-in/MLA for covariate topologies). Under topology uncertainty, ASV under candidate topologies provides worst-case sensitivity.\n\n\\paragraph{Case studies and sensitivity.}\nClinicalBERT and HumAID ($K{=}10$) confirm all patterns under linear and logistic models ($|\\Delta| < 0.02$; Appendix~\\ref{app:case_study},~\\ref{sec:correction_k_sensitivity}). End-to-end demos (Table~\\ref{tab:e2e-demo}; Appendix~\\ref{sec:real_cm_demo}) validate: PPI++ reduces exposure bias from 42.9\\% to 0.2\\%, confounding achieves 0\\% improvement (FWL), and topology-agnostic IV correction amplifies bias ($-$139\\%).\n",

ASV experiments use $C(\\delta) = (1{-}\\delta)I + \\delta C_0$.\n\n\\subsection{Bias Taxonomy}\n\\label{sec:taxonomy_results}\n\n\\paragraph{Theory-MC agreement.}\nAnalytical probability limits match Monte Carlo estimates with MAPE of 1.6\\%, averaged across 161 scenarios (23 LLM configurations $\\times$ 7 topologies; per-topology range: 0.3\\%--3.7\\%).\n\n\\paragraph{Exposure DAG: sign reversal under multi-class misclassification.}\nUnder realistic accuracy (diagonal $\\geq 0.7$), the violation rate is 13.7\\% with 2.1\\% sign reversal; at diagonal $\\geq 0.9$, violation persists at 11.9\\% (Appendix~\\ref{sec:iv_dirichlet_subspace}). Under the full Dirichlet prior, 85\\% of matrices violate the binary ``toward-null'' guarantee and 83.4\\% cause sign reversal; $\\min_k C_{kk} < 0.55$ predicts sign reversal with 91\\% accuracy (Appendix~\\ref{sec:sign_determinants}). The $K{=}2$ control confirms classical attenuation~\\cite{brenner1993}, establishing the multi-class failure as structurally distinct.\n\n\\paragraph{Structural guarantee for non-exposure topologies.}\nFive topologies exhibit zero sign-flip rate (Table~\\ref{tab:taxonomy}): confounding, mediation, and front-door show 100\\% amplification~\\cite{brenner1993, dosemeci1990} but never reverse the sign; collider is protective ($|B(C)|$ non-increasing; Conjecture~\\ref{conj:monotone}); M-bias shows weakest sensitivity (53.2\\% violation). Three ASV types (magnitude, significance, sign-flip) reveal a topology-dependent spectrum from IV (maximally fragile) to M-bias (immune, ASV $= \\infty$).}\n\\label{fig:asv_ranking}\n\\end{figure*}\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\caption{ASV summary across topologies ($K{=}3$, $\\tau{=}0.5$). ---: not applicable.}\n\\label{tab:asv_summary}\n\\begin{tabular}{lccc}\n\\toprule\n\\textbf{Topology} & \\textbf{Mag ASV} & \\textbf{Power} & \\textbf{Sign-flip} \\\\\n\\midrule\nIV          & 0.10--0.61 & 0\\%   & 1.1--6.7 \\\\\nFront-door  & 0.06--0.39 & 100\\% & --- \\\\\nConfounding & 0.08--0.49 & 100\\% & --- \\\\\nMediation   & 0.08--0.45 & 0\\%   & --- \\\\\nCollider    & 0.34--2.03 & 0\\%   & --- \\\\\nM-bias      & $\\infty$   & 98\\%  & --- \\\\\nExposure    & ---        & ---   & 83.4\\% \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n\n\\paragraph{ASV results.}\nThe ASV envelope is $\\varepsilon$-approximately monotonic for non-IV topologies ($\\varepsilon < 0.02$ for non-exposure under moderate effects, larger for exposure; Conjecture~\\ref{conj:monotone}). Figure~\\ref{fig:asv_ranking} shows the vulnerability ranking: IV is maximally fragile, M-bias is immune ($\\text{ASV} = \\infty$). Sign-flip ASV: 83.4\\% of exposure matrices yield $\\delta^*_{\\text{sf}} < 1$; IV ranges 1.1--6.7 (Table~\\ref{tab:asv_summary}). Significance ASV exhibits a phase transition at $N^* \\approx 6{,}000$--$10{,}000$; bootstrap CIs ($B{=}1{,}000$; Appendix~\\ref{sec:bootstrap_asv}) confirm stable topology ranking despite wide IV intervals.\n\n\\subsection{Empirical Validation}\n\\label{sec:empirical}\n\nWe validate using real LLM confusion matrices from HumAID~\\cite{alam2021humaid} ($K{=}10$), VAST ($K{=}3$), and CivilComments ($K{=}2$): four LLMs, 23 configurations, 161 bias scenarios.\n\n\\paragraph{Empirical bias ranking.}\nHumAID ($K{=}10$) matches predictions: IV (12.4--42.1\\%) and exposure (9.0--17.4\\%) dominate; remaining topologies $<$15\\%. $F_1$ does not exhibit significant rank correlation with bias ($\\rho = -0.10$, $p = 0.45$; Figure~\\ref{fig:f1_vs_bias}), but per-topology patterns diverge ($\\rho \\leq -0.53$ for five topologies vs.\\ $0.87$ for M-bias; Appendix~\\ref{sec:within_topo_f1}). Of exposure coefficients, 14/23 (61\\%) exhibit sign reversal, all secondary; zero across 138 non-exposure scenarios.\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{fig5_f1_vs_bias.pdf}\n\\caption{Topology determines downstream causal bias, not $F_1$. Regression calibration~\\cite{carroll2006} is designed for continuous measurement error and does not apply to categorical misclassification.\n\n\\paragraph{FWL invariance.}\nUnder linear regression, FWL invariance renders matrix-based methods (naive plug-in, MLA) provably ineffective for covariate topologies, achieving 0\\% bias reduction (Appendix~\\ref{app:fwl_proof}); under logistic regression this breaks but ranking persists ($|\\Delta| < 0.02$; Appendix~\\ref{sec:fwl_nonlinear}). To our knowledge, this futility result (that matrix-based corrections cannot reduce covariate-induced bias under FWL decomposition) has not been explicitly stated in the measurement error literature~\\cite{carroll2006, buonaccorsi2010}.\n\n\\paragraph{Method ranking.}\nPPI++ achieves the highest RMSE reduction (47.0\\% at $K{=}3$) but requires gold labels and harms M-bias ($-$88\\%); MC-SIMEX is gold-free and $K$-robust (27--70\\%), though it harms front-door ($-$62.4\\%); rankings are stable across $K$ ($\\rho \\geq 0.80$; Appendix~\\ref{sec:correction_k_sensitivity}).\n\n\\paragraph{Correction decision procedure.}\nAlgorithm~\\ref{alg:correction} (Appendix) routes through three stages: risk assessment (exposure/IV high; M-bias low), method selection (PPI++ with gold, MC-SIMEX/DSL without), and FWL screening (skip plug-in/MLA for covariate topologies). Under topology uncertainty, ASV under candidate topologies provides worst-case sensitivity.\n\n\\paragraph{Case studies and sensitivity.}\nClinicalBERT and HumAID ($K{=}10$) confirm all patterns under linear and logistic models ($|\\Delta| < 0.02$; Appendix~\\ref{app:case_study},~\\ref{sec:correction_k_sensitivity}). End-to-end demos (Table~\\ref{tab:e2e-demo}; Appendix~\\ref{sec:real_cm_demo}) validate: PPI++ reduces exposure bias from 42.9\\% to 0.2\\%, confounding achieves 0\\% improvement (FWL), and topology-agnostic IV correction amplifies bias ($-$139\\%).\n",

Collider 100\\% amplification reflects Berkson bias; misclassification is protective.}\n\\label{tab:taxonomy}\n\\small\n\\resizebox{\\columnwidth}{!}{\\begin{tabular}{lcccc}\n\\toprule\n\\textbf{Topology} & \\textbf{Violation} & \\textbf{Sign Flip} & \\textbf{Amplif.} & $\\tau_{\\text{sf}}{=}0$? \\\\\n\\midrule\nConfounding & 100\\% & 0\\% & 100\\% & Cond. \\\\\nExposure & 85\\% & 83.4\\% & 14.1\\% & No \\\\\nMediation & 100\\% & 0\\% & 100\\% & Cond. \\\\\nFront-door & 100\\% & 0\\% & 100\\% & Cond. \\\\\nCollider & 100\\% & 0\\% & 100\\% & Cond. \\\\\nM-bias & 53.2\\% & 0\\% & 10.4\\% & Yes \\\\\nIV & 100\\% & 52.6\\% & 100\\% & No \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}}\n\\end{table}\n\nThe bias distributions are visualized in Figure~\\ref{fig:bias_distribution}.\n\n\\subsection{ASV Framework Validation}\n\\label{sec:asv_results}\n\n\\begin{figure*}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=0.85\\textwidth]{fig3_asv_ranking.pdf}\n\\caption{ASV vulnerability ranking across topologies. Three ASV types (magnitude, significance, sign-flip) reveal a topology-dependent spectrum from IV (maximally fragile) to M-bias (immune, ASV $= \\infty$).}\n\\label{fig:asv_ranking}\n\\end{figure*}\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\caption{ASV summary across topologies ($K{=}3$, $\\tau{=}0.5$). ---: not applicable.}\n\\label{tab:asv_summary}\n\\begin{tabular}{lccc}\n\\toprule\n\\textbf{Topology} & \\textbf{Mag ASV} & \\textbf{Power} & \\textbf{Sign-flip} \\\\\n\\midrule\nIV          & 0.10--0.61 & 0\\%   & 1.1--6.7 \\\\\nFront-door  & 0.06--0.39 & 100\\% & --- \\\\\nConfounding & 0.08--0.49 & 100\\% & --- \\\\\nMediation   & 0.08--0.45 & 0\\%   & --- \\\\\nCollider    & 0.34--2.03 & 0\\%   & --- \\\\\nM-bias      & $\\infty$   & 98\\%  & --- \\\\\nExposure    & ---        & ---   & 83.4\\% \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n\n\\paragraph{ASV results.}\nThe ASV envelope is $\\varepsilon$-approximately monotonic for non-IV topologies ($\\varepsilon < 0.02$ for non-exposure under moderate effects, larger for exposure; Conjecture~\\ref{conj:monotone}). Figure~\\ref{fig:asv_ranking} shows the vulnerability ranking: IV is maximally fragile, M-bias is immune ($\\text{ASV} = \\infty$). Sign-flip ASV: 83.4\\% of exposure matrices yield $\\delta^*_{\\text{sf}} < 1$; IV ranges 1.1--6.7 (Table~\\ref{tab:asv_summary}). Significance ASV exhibits a phase transition at $N^* \\approx 6{,}000$--$10{,}000$; bootstrap CIs ($B{=}1{,}000$; Appendix~\\ref{sec:bootstrap_asv}) confirm stable topology ranking despite wide IV intervals.\n\n\\subsection{Empirical Validation}\n\\label{sec:empirical}\n\nWe validate using real LLM confusion matrices from HumAID~\\cite{alam2021humaid} ($K{=}10$), VAST ($K{=}3$), and CivilComments ($K{=}2$): four LLMs, 23 configurations, 161 bias scenarios.\n\n\\paragraph{Empirical bias ranking.}\nHumAID ($K{=}10$) matches predictions: IV (12.4--42.1\\%) and exposure (9.0--17.4\\%) dominate; remaining topologies $<$15\\%. $F_1$ does not exhibit significant rank correlation with bias ($\\rho = -0.10$, $p = 0.45$; Figure~\\ref{fig:f1_vs_bias}), but per-topology patterns diverge ($\\rho \\leq -0.53$ for five topologies vs.\\ $0.87$ for M-bias; Appendix~\\ref{sec:within_topo_f1}). Of exposure coefficients, 14/23 (61\\%) exhibit sign reversal, all secondary; zero across 138 non-exposure scenarios.\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{fig5_f1_vs_bias.pdf}\n\\caption{Topology determines downstream causal bias, not $F_1$. HumAID $K{=}10$ (56 points): within $F_1$ range 0.614--0.690, bias varies $10{\\times}$ (IV 42.1\\% vs.\\ collider $<$5.6\\%).}\n\\label{fig:f1_vs_bias}\n\\end{figure}\n\n\n\\subsection{Correction Guidance}\n\\label{sec:correction}\n\nWe evaluate five correction methods (naive plug-in, MLA, DSL~\\cite{egami_hinck_stewart_wei2023}, MC-SIMEX~\\cite{kuchenhoff2006simex}, PPI++~\\cite{angelopoulos2023}) across all topologies (210 configurations; Table~\\ref{tab:correction}, Appendix~\\ref{app:supplementary}). Regression calibration~\\cite{carroll2006} is designed for continuous measurement error and does not apply to categorical misclassification.\n\n\\paragraph{FWL invariance.}\nUnder linear regression, FWL invariance renders matrix-based methods (naive plug-in, MLA) provably ineffective for covariate topologies, achieving 0\\% bias reduction (Appendix~\\ref{app:fwl_proof}); under logistic regression this breaks but ranking persists ($|\\Delta| < 0.02$; Appendix~\\ref{sec:fwl_nonlinear}). To our knowledge, this futility result (that matrix-based corrections cannot reduce covariate-induced bias under FWL decomposition) has not been explicitly stated in the measurement error literature~\\cite{carroll2006, buonaccorsi2010}.\n\n\\paragraph{Method ranking.}\nPPI++ achieves the highest RMSE reduction (47.0\\% at $K{=}3$) but requires gold labels and harms M-bias ($-$88\\%); MC-SIMEX is gold-free and $K$-robust (27--70\\%), though it harms front-door ($-$62.4\\%); rankings are stable across $K$ ($\\rho \\geq 0.80$; Appendix~\\ref{sec:correction_k_sensitivity}).\n\n\\paragraph{Correction decision procedure.}\nAlgorithm~\\ref{alg:correction} (Appendix) routes through three stages: risk assessment (exposure/IV high; M-bias low), method selection (PPI++ with gold, MC-SIMEX/DSL without), and FWL screening (skip plug-in/MLA for covariate topologies). Under topology uncertainty, ASV under candidate topologies provides worst-case sensitivity.\n\n\\paragraph{Case studies and sensitivity.}\nClinicalBERT and HumAID ($K{=}10$) confirm all patterns under linear and logistic models ($|\\Delta| < 0.02$; Appendix~\\ref{app:case_study},~\\ref{sec:correction_k_sensitivity}). End-to-end demos (Table~\\ref{tab:e2e-demo}; Appendix~\\ref{sec:real_cm_demo}) validate: PPI++ reduces exposure bias from 42.9\\% to 0.2\\%, confounding achieves 0\\% improvement (FWL), and topology-agnostic IV correction amplifies bias ($-$139\\%).\n",

    "originalFile": "\\section{Experiments}\n\\label{sec:experiments}\n\nWe validate through Monte Carlo simulation ($K{=}3$), cross-$K$ robustness checks ($K{=}5,7$), and empirical validation with real LLM confusion matrices from three natural language processing (NLP) tasks ($K{=}2,3,10$).\n\n\\subsection{Setup}\n\\label{sec:setup}\n\nFor each topology, we sample 100{,}000 confusion matrices from a Dirichlet$(1,1,1)$ prior, compute analytical probability limits, and validate against Monte Carlo simulation ($N{=}10{,}000$). ASV experiments use $C(\\delta) = (1{-}\\delta)I + \\delta C_0$.\n\n\\subsection{Bias Taxonomy}\n\\label{sec:taxonomy_results}\n\n\\paragraph{Theory-MC agreement.}\nAnalytical probability limits match Monte Carlo estimates with mean absolute percentage error (MAPE) of 1.6\\%, averaged across 161 scenarios (23 LLM configurations $\\times$ 7 topologies; per-topology range: 0.3\\%--3.7\\%).\n\n\\paragraph{Exposure DAG: sign reversal under multi-class misclassification.}\nUnder realistic accuracy (diagonal $\\geq 0.7$), the violation rate is 13.7\\% with 2.1\\% sign reversal; at diagonal $\\geq 0.9$, violation persists at 11.9\\% (Appendix~\\ref{sec:iv_dirichlet_subspace}). Under the full Dirichlet prior, 85\\% of matrices violate the binary ``toward-null'' guarantee and 83.4\\% cause sign reversal; $\\min_k C_{kk} < 0.55$ predicts sign reversal with 91\\% accuracy (Appendix~\\ref{sec:sign_determinants}). The $K{=}2$ control confirms classical attenuation~\\cite{brenner1993}, establishing the multi-class failure as structurally distinct.\n\n\\paragraph{Structural guarantee for non-exposure topologies.}\nFive topologies exhibit zero sign-flip rate (Table~\\ref{tab:taxonomy}): confounding, mediation, and front-door show 100\\% amplification~\\cite{brenner1993, dosemeci1990} but never reverse the sign; collider is protective ($|B(C)|$ non-increasing; Conjecture~\\ref{conj:monotone}); M-bias shows weakest sensitivity (53.2\\% violation). All patterns persist at $K{=}5,7$ (Appendix~\\ref{sec:cross_k_robustness}).\n\n\\paragraph{IV: Wald estimator instability.}\nThe IV topology is the sole non-exposure exception: 52.6\\% of random matrices cause sign reversal, stable across $K{=}3,5,7$. Diagonal dominance ($C_{kk} > 0.5$) eliminates sign reversal but preserves amplification (Appendix~\\ref{sec:iv_dirichlet_subspace}); however, all 23 empirical LLM configurations show zero IV sign reversal.\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\caption{Bias under $K{=}3$ non-differential misclassification (100{,}000 Dirichlet$(1,1,1)$ matrices). Violation: $|\\text{bias}| > 0$; Sign Flip: any coefficient reverses sign; Amplif.: $|\\hat{\\tau}| > |\\tau|$. $\\tau_{\\text{sf}}{=}0$?: sign-flip guarantee holds unconditionally (Yes), conditionally (Cond.; Proposition~\\ref{prop:signflip}), or not (No). Collider 100\\% amplification reflects Berkson bias; misclassification is protective.}\n\\label{tab:taxonomy}\n\\small\n\\resizebox{\\columnwidth}{!}{\\begin{tabular}{lcccc}\n\\toprule\n\\textbf{Topology} & \\textbf{Violation} & \\textbf{Sign Flip} & \\textbf{Amplif.} & $\\tau_{\\text{sf}}{=}0$? \\\\\n\\midrule\nConfounding & 100\\% & 0\\% & 100\\% & Cond. \\\\\nExposure & 85\\% & 83.4\\% & 14.1\\% & No \\\\\nMediation & 100\\% & 0\\% & 100\\% & Cond. \\\\\nFront-door & 100\\% & 0\\% & 100\\% & Cond. \\\\\nCollider & 100\\% & 0\\% & 100\\% & Cond. \\\\\nM-bias & 53.2\\% & 0\\% & 10.4\\% & Yes \\\\\nIV & 100\\% & 52.6\\% & 100\\% & No \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}}\n\\end{table}\n\nThe bias distributions are visualized in Figure~\\ref{fig:bias_distribution}.\n\n\\subsection{ASV Framework Validation}\n\\label{sec:asv_results}\n\n\\begin{figure*}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=0.85\\textwidth]{fig3_asv_ranking.pdf}\n\\caption{ASV vulnerability ranking across topologies. Three ASV types (magnitude, significance, sign-flip) reveal a topology-dependent spectrum from IV (maximally fragile) to M-bias (immune, ASV $= \\infty$).}\n\\label{fig:asv_ranking}\n\\end{figure*}\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\caption{ASV summary across topologies ($K{=}3$, $\\tau{=}0.5$). ---: not applicable.}\n\\label{tab:asv_summary}\n\\begin{tabular}{lccc}\n\\toprule\n\\textbf{Topology} & \\textbf{Mag ASV} & \\textbf{Power} & \\textbf{Sign-flip} \\\\\n\\midrule\nIV          & 0.10--0.61 & 0\\%   & 1.1--6.7 \\\\\nFront-door  & 0.06--0.39 & 100\\% & --- \\\\\nConfounding & 0.08--0.49 & 100\\% & --- \\\\\nMediation   & 0.08--0.45 & 0\\%   & --- \\\\\nCollider    & 0.34--2.03 & 0\\%   & --- \\\\\nM-bias      & $\\infty$   & 98\\%  & --- \\\\\nExposure    & ---        & ---   & 83.4\\% \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n\n\\paragraph{ASV results.}\nThe ASV envelope is $\\varepsilon$-approximately monotonic for non-IV topologies ($\\varepsilon < 0.02$ for non-exposure under moderate effects, larger for exposure; Conjecture~\\ref{conj:monotone}). Figure~\\ref{fig:asv_ranking} shows the vulnerability ranking: IV is maximally fragile, M-bias is immune ($\\text{ASV} = \\infty$). Sign-flip ASV: 83.4\\% of exposure matrices yield $\\delta^*_{\\text{sf}} < 1$; IV ranges 1.1--6.7 (Table~\\ref{tab:asv_summary}). Significance ASV exhibits a phase transition at $N^* \\approx 6{,}000$--$10{,}000$; bootstrap CIs ($B{=}1{,}000$; Appendix~\\ref{sec:bootstrap_asv}) confirm stable topology ranking despite wide IV intervals.\n\n\\subsection{Empirical Validation}\n\\label{sec:empirical}\n\nWe validate using real LLM confusion matrices from HumAID~\\cite{alam2021humaid} ($K{=}10$), VAST ($K{=}3$), and CivilComments ($K{=}2$): four LLMs, 23 configurations, 161 bias scenarios.\n\n\\paragraph{Empirical bias ranking.}\nHumAID ($K{=}10$) matches predictions: IV (12.4--42.1\\%) and exposure (9.0--17.4\\%) dominate; remaining topologies $<$15\\%. $F_1$ does not exhibit significant rank correlation with bias ($\\rho = -0.10$, $p = 0.45$; Figure~\\ref{fig:f1_vs_bias}), but per-topology patterns diverge ($\\rho \\leq -0.53$ for five topologies vs.\\ $0.87$ for M-bias; Appendix~\\ref{sec:within_topo_f1}). Of exposure coefficients, 14/23 (61\\%) exhibit sign reversal, all secondary; zero across 138 non-exposure scenarios.\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{fig5_f1_vs_bias.pdf}\n\\caption{Topology determines downstream causal bias, not $F_1$. HumAID $K{=}10$ (56 points): within $F_1$ range 0.614--0.690, bias varies $10{\\times}$ (IV 42.1\\% vs.\\ collider $<$5.6\\%).}\n\\label{fig:f1_vs_bias}\n\\end{figure}\n\n\n\\subsection{Correction Guidance}\n\\label{sec:correction}\n\nWe evaluate five correction methods (naive plug-in, maximum likelihood adjustment (MLA), design-based supervised learning (DSL)~\\cite{egami_hinck_stewart_wei2023}, Monte Carlo simulation extrapolation (MC-SIMEX)~\\cite{kuchenhoff2006simex}, Prediction-Powered Inference++ (PPI++)~\\cite{angelopoulos2023}) across all topologies (210 configurations; Table~\\ref{tab:correction}, Appendix~\\ref{app:supplementary}). Regression calibration~\\cite{carroll2006} is designed for continuous measurement error and does not apply to categorical misclassification.\n\n\\paragraph{FWL invariance.}\nUnder linear regression, FWL invariance renders matrix-based methods (naive plug-in, MLA) provably ineffective for covariate topologies, achieving 0\\% bias reduction (Appendix~\\ref{app:fwl_proof}); under logistic regression this breaks but ranking persists ($|\\Delta| < 0.02$; Appendix~\\ref{sec:fwl_nonlinear}). To our knowledge, this futility result (that matrix-based corrections cannot reduce covariate-induced bias under FWL decomposition) has not been explicitly stated in the measurement error literature~\\cite{carroll2006, buonaccorsi2010}.\n\n\\paragraph{Method ranking.}\nPPI++ achieves the highest root mean squared error (RMSE) reduction (47.0\\% at $K{=}3$) but requires gold labels and harms M-bias ($-$88\\%); MC-SIMEX is gold-free and $K$-robust (27--70\\%), though it harms front-door ($-$62.4\\%); rankings are stable across $K$ ($\\rho \\geq 0.80$; Appendix~\\ref{sec:correction_k_sensitivity}).\n\n\\paragraph{Correction decision procedure.}\nAlgorithm~\\ref{alg:correction} (Appendix) routes through three stages: risk assessment (exposure/IV high; M-bias low), method selection (PPI++ with gold, MC-SIMEX/DSL without), and FWL screening (skip plug-in/MLA for covariate topologies). Under topology uncertainty, ASV under candidate topologies provides worst-case sensitivity.\n\n\\paragraph{Case studies and sensitivity.}\nClinicalBERT and HumAID ($K{=}10$) confirm all patterns under linear and logistic models ($|\\Delta| < 0.02$; Appendix~\\ref{app:case_study},~\\ref{sec:correction_k_sensitivity}). End-to-end demos (Table~\\ref{tab:e2e-demo}; Appendix~\\ref{sec:real_cm_demo}) validate: PPI++ reduces exposure bias from 42.9\\% to 0.2\\%, confounding achieves 0\\% improvement (FWL), and topology-agnostic IV correction amplifies bias ($-$139\\%).\n",

<details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_01JRhaj9giNZbzga9iEzf2ky`</summary>

```text
1	\begin{abstract}
2	When large language model (LLM)-generated annotations feed into causal inference, annotation quality is the wrong thing to worry about; the causal directed acyclic graph (DAG) topology determines whether errors attenuate, amplify, or reverse the treatment effect estimate. We derive bias expressions for seven canonical topologies under $K$-class non-differential misclassification in linear structural equation models and show that $K {\geq} 3$ introduces a structural possibility of sign reversal absent in the binary case. Under proportional effects, five topologies preserve the treatment effect sign under regularity conditions; exposure and instrumental variable (IV) can reverse it. Under empirical LLM confusion matrices, sign reversal is confined to exposure topology. We introduce Annotation Sensitivity Values (ASVs), with proved ($K{=}2$, $K{=}3$ symmetric) and extensively validated (general $K{\geq}3$) monotonicity that yields a vulnerability ranking from IV (maximally fragile) to M-bias (immune), and show that matrix-based corrections are provably ineffective under linear regression for four topologies due to Frisch--Waugh--Lovell invariance. A case study with 10 real LLM confusion matrices confirms: topology-aware correction reduces exposure bias from 61\% to $<$1\%, while topology-blind corrections are provably futile under linear regression for four topologies.
3	\end{abstract}
4	
```

<details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_01J7e2fYtSWkf5CemReSQoBo`</summary>

```text
11	Our ASV framework draws on E-values~\cite{vanderweele_ding2017, mathur2018}, robustness values~\cite{cinelli2020making}, and mediation sensitivity~\cite{imai_keele_yamamoto2010, ding_vanderweele2016, pryzant_etal2021}, but parametrizes annotation error severity rather than omitted variable strength. \citet{lash2009}, \citet{blackwell_honaker_king2017}, \citet{mahajan2006}, and \citet{hu2008} provide complementary frameworks for misclassified regressors. For correction, PPI++~\cite{angelopoulos2023} and DSL~\cite{egami_hinck_stewart_wei2023} are topology-agnostic~\cite{zrnic_candes2024, demirel2024prediction, cadei2025, gligoric2025}. We show this agnosticism is costly: correction effectiveness is topology-dependent, with matrix-based methods provably ineffective under FWL decomposition for four topologies.
```

    "newString": "For correction, Prediction-Powered Inference++ (PPI++)~\\cite{angelopoulos2023} and design-based supervised learning (DSL)~\\cite{egami_hinck_stewart_wei2023} are topology-agnostic",
    "originalFile": "\\section{Related Work}\n\\label{sec:related_work}\n\n\\paragraph{Measurement error in causal inference.}\nFoundations appear in \\citet{carroll2006, gustafson2004, buonaccorsi2010, bound2001measurement}, with DAG-based formalization by \\citet{hernan_cole2009} and \\citet{kuroki_pearl2014}. For binary variables, non-differential misclassification biases toward the null~\\cite{bross1954misclassification, brenner1993, ogburn_vanderweele2012}, but this fails for polytomous exposures~\\cite{dosemeci1990, jurek2005, veirod_laake2001}. Extensions address differential error~\\cite{edwards_lessler_naimi2021, imai_yamamoto2010}, mismeasured covariates~\\cite{shu_yi2019a, shu_yi2019b}, mediation~\\cite{valeri_vanderweele2013}, and nonclassical error~\\cite{schennach2020mismeasured, nguimkeu_denteh_tchernis2019, wei2025multiply}. All analyze a single DAG; ours characterizes how the same confusion matrix produces qualitatively different bias across seven topologies.\n\n\\paragraph{Text classifiers and LLM annotation.}\n\\citet{grimmer2022} and \\citet{feder_etal2022} provide foundations for text-as-data causal inference. \\citet{wood_doughty2018} first applied DAG reasoning to classifier error; \\citet{teblunthuis2024} gave the most complete binary treatment ($K{=}2$, four DAGs); \\citet{lee_wood_doughty2024} extended to categorical LLM classifiers but covered only confounding. On annotation quality, \\citet{gilardi2023chatgpt} and \\citet{ziems2024can} document rapid LLM adoption~\\cite{tan2024llm}, \\citet{baumann2025} found 31\\% incorrect conclusions, and \\citet{kim_garg2025} showed 60\\% error correlation~\\cite{pangakis_wolken_fasching2023, cheng_mayya_sedoc2024, audinet2025, keith_jensen_oconnor2020, variance_aware_llm2026, hidden_measurement_error2026}. These works either restrict to binary labels, cover a single topology, or diagnose quality without connecting it to causal validity.\n\n\\paragraph{Sensitivity analysis and correction methods.}\nOur ASV framework draws on E-values~\\cite{vanderweele_ding2017, mathur2018}, robustness values~\\cite{cinelli2020making}, and mediation sensitivity~\\cite{imai_keele_yamamoto2010, ding_vanderweele2016, pryzant_etal2021}, but parametrizes annotation error severity rather than omitted variable strength. \\citet{lash2009}, \\citet{blackwell_honaker_king2017}, \\citet{mahajan2006}, and \\citet{hu2008} provide complementary frameworks for misclassified regressors. For correction, PPI++~\\cite{angelopoulos2023} and DSL~\\cite{egami_hinck_stewart_wei2023} are topology-agnostic~\\cite{zrnic_candes2024, demirel2024prediction, cadei2025, gligoric2025}. We show this agnosticism is costly: correction effectiveness is topology-dependent, with matrix-based methods provably ineffective under FWL decomposition for four topologies.\n",

  "toolUseResult": {
    "filePath": "/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-prod_0510/projects/causal_annot_error_attrib/docs/paper/experiments.tex",
    "oldString": "We evaluate five correction methods (naive plug-in, maximum likelihood adjustment (MLA), design-based supervised learning (DSL)~\\cite{egami_hinck_stewart_wei2023}, Monte Carlo simulation extrapolation (MC-SIMEX)~\\cite{kuchenhoff2006simex}, Prediction-Powered Inference++ (PPI++)~\\cite{angelopoulos2023}) across all topologies",
    "newString": "We evaluate five correction methods (naive plug-in, maximum likelihood adjustment (MLA), DSL~\\cite{egami_hinck_stewart_wei2023}, Monte Carlo simulation extrapolation (MC-SIMEX)~\\cite{kuchenhoff2006simex}, PPI++~\\cite{angelopoulos2023}) across all topologies",
    "originalFile": "\\section{Experiments}\n\\label{sec:experiments}\n\nWe validate through Monte Carlo simulation ($K{=}3$), cross-$K$ robustness checks ($K{=}5,7$), and empirical validation with real LLM confusion matrices from three natural language processing (NLP) tasks ($K{=}2,3,10$).\n\n\\subsection{Setup}\n\\label{sec:setup}\n\nFor each topology, we sample 100{,}000 confusion matrices from a Dirichlet$(1,1,1)$ prior, compute analytical probability limits, and validate against Monte Carlo simulation ($N{=}10{,}000$). ASV experiments use $C(\\delta) = (1{-}\\delta)I + \\delta C_0$.\n\n\\subsection{Bias Taxonomy}\n\\label{sec:taxonomy_results}\n\n\\paragraph{Theory-MC agreement.}\nAnalytical probability limits match Monte Carlo estimates with mean absolute percentage error (MAPE) of 1.6\\%, averaged across 161 scenarios (23 LLM configurations $\\times$ 7 topologies; per-topology range: 0.3\\%--3.7\\%).\n\n\\paragraph{Exposure DAG: sign reversal under multi-class misclassification.}\nUnder realistic accuracy (diagonal $\\geq 0.7$), the violation rate is 13.7\\% with 2.1\\% sign reversal; at diagonal $\\geq 0.9$, violation persists at 11.9\\% (Appendix~\\ref{sec:iv_dirichlet_subspace}). Under the full Dirichlet prior, 85\\% of matrices violate the binary ``toward-null'' guarantee and 83.4\\% cause sign reversal; $\\min_k C_{kk} < 0.55$ predicts sign reversal with 91\\% accuracy (Appendix~\\ref{sec:sign_determinants}). The $K{=}2$ control confirms classical attenuation~\\cite{brenner1993}, establishing the multi-class failure as structurally distinct.\n\n\\paragraph{Structural guarantee for non-exposure topologies.}\nFive topologies exhibit zero sign-flip rate (Table~\\ref{tab:taxonomy}): confounding, mediation, and front-door show 100\\% amplification~\\cite{brenner1993, dosemeci1990} but never reverse the sign; collider is protective ($|B(C)|$ non-increasing; Conjecture~\\ref{conj:monotone}); M-bias shows weakest sensitivity (53.2\\% violation). All patterns persist at $K{=}5,7$ (Appendix~\\ref{sec:cross_k_robustness}).\n\n\\paragraph{IV: Wald estimator instability.}\nThe IV topology is the sole non-exposure exception: 52.6\\% of random matrices cause sign reversal, stable across $K{=}3,5,7$. Diagonal dominance ($C_{kk} > 0.5$) eliminates sign reversal but preserves amplification (Appendix~\\ref{sec:iv_dirichlet_subspace}); however, all 23 empirical LLM configurations show zero IV sign reversal.\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\caption{Bias under $K{=}3$ non-differential misclassification (100{,}000 Dirichlet$(1,1,1)$ matrices). Violation: $|\\text{bias}| > 0$; Sign Flip: any coefficient reverses sign; Amplif.: $|\\hat{\\tau}| > |\\tau|$. $\\tau_{\\text{sf}}{=}0$?: sign-flip guarantee holds unconditionally (Yes), conditionally (Cond.; Proposition~\\ref{prop:signflip}), or not (No). Collider 100\\% amplification reflects Berkson bias; misclassification is protective.}\n\\label{tab:taxonomy}\n\\small\n\\resizebox{\\columnwidth}{!}{\\begin{tabular}{lcccc}\n\\toprule\n\\textbf{Topology} & \\textbf{Violation} & \\textbf{Sign Flip} & \\textbf{Amplif.} & $\\tau_{\\text{sf}}{=}0$? \\\\\n\\midrule\nConfounding & 100\\% & 0\\% & 100\\% & Cond. \\\\\nExposure & 85\\% & 83.4\\% & 14.1\\% & No \\\\\nMediation & 100\\% & 0\\% & 100\\% & Cond. \\\\\nFront-door & 100\\% & 0\\% & 100\\% & Cond. \\\\\nCollider & 100\\% & 0\\% & 100\\% & Cond. \\\\\nM-bias & 53.2\\% & 0\\% & 10.4\\% & Yes \\\\\nIV & 100\\% & 52.6\\% & 100\\% & No \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}}\n\\end{table}\n\nThe bias distributions are visualized in Figure~\\ref{fig:bias_distribution}.\n\n\\subsection{ASV Framework Validation}\n\\label{sec:asv_results}\n\n\\begin{figure*}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=0.85\\textwidth]{fig3_asv_ranking.pdf}\n\\caption{ASV vulnerability ranking across topologies. Three ASV types (magnitude, significance, sign-flip) reveal a topology-dependent spectrum from IV (maximally fragile) to M-bias (immune, ASV $= \\infty$).}\n\\label{fig:asv_ranking}\n\\end{figure*}\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\caption{ASV summary across topologies ($K{=}3$, $\\tau{=}0.5$). ---: not applicable.}\n\\label{tab:asv_summary}\n\\begin{tabular}{lccc}\n\\toprule\n\\textbf{Topology} & \\textbf{Mag ASV} & \\textbf{Power} & \\textbf{Sign-flip} \\\\\n\\midrule\nIV          & 0.10--0.61 & 0\\%   & 1.1--6.7 \\\\\nFront-door  & 0.06--0.39 & 100\\% & --- \\\\\nConfounding & 0.08--0.49 & 100\\% & --- \\\\\nMediation   & 0.08--0.45 & 0\\%   & --- \\\\\nCollider    & 0.34--2.03 & 0\\%   & --- \\\\\nM-bias      & $\\infty$   & 98\\%  & --- \\\\\nExposure    & ---        & ---   & 83.4\\% \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n\n\\paragraph{ASV results.}\nThe ASV envelope is $\\varepsilon$-approximately monotonic for non-IV topologies ($\\varepsilon < 0.02$ for non-exposure under moderate effects, larger for exposure; Conjecture~\\ref{conj:monotone}). Figure~\\ref{fig:asv_ranking} shows the vulnerability ranking: IV is maximally fragile, M-bias is immune ($\\text{ASV} = \\infty$). Sign-flip ASV: 83.4\\% of exposure matrices yield $\\delta^*_{\\text{sf}} < 1$; IV ranges 1.1--6.7 (Table~\\ref{tab:asv_summary}). Significance ASV exhibits a phase transition at $N^* \\approx 6{,}000$--$10{,}000$; bootstrap confidence intervals (CIs; $B{=}1{,}000$; Appendix~\\ref{sec:bootstrap_asv}) confirm stable topology ranking despite wide IV intervals.\n\n\\subsection{Empirical Validation}\n\\label{sec:empirical}\n\nWe validate using real LLM confusion matrices from HumAID~\\cite{alam2021humaid} ($K{=}10$), VAST ($K{=}3$), and CivilComments ($K{=}2$): four LLMs, 23 configurations, 161 bias scenarios.\n\n\\paragraph{Empirical bias ranking.}\nHumAID ($K{=}10$) matches predictions: IV (12.4--42.1\\%) and exposure (9.0--17.4\\%) dominate; remaining topologies $<$15\\%. $F_1$ does not exhibit significant rank correlation with bias ($\\rho = -0.10$, $p = 0.45$; Figure~\\ref{fig:f1_vs_bias}), but per-topology patterns diverge ($\\rho \\leq -0.53$ for five topologies vs.\\ $0.87$ for M-bias; Appendix~\\ref{sec:within_topo_f1}). Of exposure coefficients, 14/23 (61\\%) exhibit sign reversal, all secondary; zero across 138 non-exposure scenarios.\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{fig5_f1_vs_bias.pdf}\n\\caption{Topology determines downstream causal bias, not $F_1$. HumAID $K{=}10$ (56 points): within $F_1$ range 0.614--0.690, bias varies $10{\\times}$ (IV 42.1\\% vs.\\ collider $<$5.6\\%).}\n\\label{fig:f1_vs_bias}\n\\end{figure}\n\n\n\\subsection{Correction Guidance}\n\\label{sec:correction}\n\nWe evaluate five correction methods (naive plug-in, maximum likelihood adjustment (MLA), design-based supervised learning (DSL)~\\cite{egami_hinck_stewart_wei2023}, Monte Carlo simulation extrapolation (MC-SIMEX)~\\cite{kuchenhoff2006simex}, Prediction-Powered Inference++ (PPI++)~\\cite{angelopoulos2023}) across all topologies (210 configurations; Table~\\ref{tab:correction}, Appendix~\\ref{app:supplementary}). Regression calibration~\\cite{carroll2006} is designed for continuous measurement error and does not apply to categorical misclassification.\n\n\\paragraph{FWL invariance.}\nUnder linear regression, FWL invariance renders matrix-based methods (naive plug-in, MLA) provably ineffective for covariate topologies, achieving 0\\% bias reduction (Appendix~\\ref{app:fwl_proof}); under logistic regression this breaks but ranking persists ($|\\Delta| < 0.02$; Appendix~\\ref{sec:fwl_nonlinear}). To our knowledge, this futility result (that matrix-based corrections cannot reduce covariate-induced bias under FWL decomposition) has not been explicitly stated in the measurement error literature~\\cite{carroll2006, buonaccorsi2010}.\n\n\\paragraph{Method ranking.}\nPPI++ achieves the highest root mean squared error (RMSE) reduction (47.0\\% at $K{=}3$) but requires gold labels and harms M-bias ($-$88\\%); MC-SIMEX is gold-free and $K$-robust (27--70\\%), though it harms front-door ($-$62.4\\%); rankings are stable across $K$ ($\\rho \\geq 0.80$; Appendix~\\ref{sec:correction_k_sensitivity}).\n\n\\paragraph{Correction decision procedure.}\nAlgorithm~\\ref{alg:correction} (Appendix) routes through three stages: risk assessment (exposure/IV high; M-bias low), method selection (PPI++ with gold, MC-SIMEX/DSL without), and FWL screening (skip plug-in/MLA for covariate topologies). Under topology uncertainty, ASV under candidate topologies provides worst-case sensitivity.\n\n\\paragraph{Case studies and sensitivity.}\nClinicalBERT and HumAID ($K{=}10$) confirm all patterns under linear and logistic models ($|\\Delta| < 0.02$; Appendix~\\ref{app:case_study},~\\ref{sec:correction_k_sensitivity}). End-to-end demos (Table~\\ref{tab:e2e-demo}; Appendix~\\ref{sec:real_cm_demo}) validate: PPI++ reduces exposure bias from 42.9\\% to 0.2\\%, confounding achieves 0\\% improvement (FWL), and topology-agnostic IV correction amplifies bias ($-$139\\%).\n",

### Tool result

_source line 3460 · 2026-05-24T18:31:17.289Z_

<details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_01HbB41CNpw725JGBawEcBBm`</summary>

```text
974	For the four topologies where $A$ enters as a covariate (confounding, mediation, collider, M-bias), the regression is $Y = \tau T + \boldsymbol{\gamma}^\top \mathbf{D}^* + \varepsilon$, where $\mathbf{D}^*$ is the $N \times (K{-}1)$ dummy matrix for $A^*$. Any matrix-based correction replaces $\mathbf{D}^*$ with $\tilde{\mathbf{D}} = \mathbf{D}^* M$ for some invertible $(K{-}1) \times (K{-}1)$ matrix $M$ (e.g., $M = C^{-1}$ for plug-in, or $M = (C^\top C)^{-1} C^\top$ for MLE). By the Frisch--Waugh--Lovell theorem~\cite{lovell1963}, $\hat{\tau}$ depends only on the residual of $T$ projected off $\operatorname{col}(\mathbf{D}^*)$. Since $\operatorname{col}(\mathbf{D}^* M) = \operatorname{col}(\mathbf{D}^*)$ for invertible $M$, the projection is unchanged and $\hat{\tau}(\tilde{\mathbf{D}}) = \hat{\tau}(\mathbf{D}^*)$ exactly. SIMEX adds noise $\mathbf{D}^*_{\text{sim}} = \mathbf{D}^* + \lambda \mathbf{E}$; when $\mathbf{E}$ is generated from the same column space, the extrapolation to $\lambda = -1$ converges to a column-space-equivalent transformation, yielding 0\% bias reduction empirically.
975	
976	\paragraph{FWL breakdown under logistic regression.}
977	\label{sec:fwl_logistic}
978	The column-space argument applies only to estimators whose treatment coefficient is a function of $T$ residualized off $\operatorname{col}(\mathbf{D}^*)$---a property of linear projection. The logistic MLE solves $\sum_i (Y_i - \sigma(\mathbf{x}_i^\top\boldsymbol{\beta}))\mathbf{x}_i = \mathbf{0}$; the implicit weighting by $\sigma'(\cdot)$ depends on the actual values of the design columns, not only on their span, so replacing $\mathbf{D}^*$ by $\mathbf{D}^* M$ generally changes the fitted $\hat\tau$. We verify this on the confounding topology ($K{=}3$, $\tau{=}0.5$, $N{=}10{,}000\times 500$ MC reps) under both linear ($Y = \tau T + \mathbf{D}\boldsymbol{\beta}+\varepsilon$) and logistic ($Y\sim\mathrm{Bern}(\sigma(\tau T + \mathbf{D}\boldsymbol{\beta}))$) DGPs, comparing the uncorrected estimator (fit on $\mathbf{D}^*$) against Naive plug-in (fit on per-sample $(C^{-1}\mathbf{e}_{A^*})_{1:K}$). Per-rep estimates are paired (same $A^*$, same outcome).
```

```text
87	We define three complementary measures: (1)~\textbf{Magnitude ASV} $\delta^*_{\text{mag}} = \min\{\delta : |\operatorname{plim}\;\hat{\tau}(C(\delta)) - \tau| > \epsilon\}$, analogous to E-values; (2)~\textbf{Significance ASV} $\delta^*_{\text{sig}} = \min\{\delta : |\operatorname{plim}\;\hat{\tau}(C(\delta))| < z_{\alpha/2} \cdot SE(N)\}$, relevant for attenuating topologies; (3)~\textbf{Sign-flip ASV} $\delta^*_{\text{sf}} = \min\{\delta : \operatorname{sign}(\hat{\tau}) \neq \operatorname{sign}(\tau)\}$, for exposure and IV only. The significance ASV depends on $N$ through $SE(N)$, exhibiting a sharp phase transition at a critical $N^*$ below which all rejections are blocked.
```

The user (周俊伟) has three requests:

1. Rewrite Related Work section to meet AI top-conference standards (not just a stack of citations, but with clear narrative purpose)
2. Add at least 2 more figures to the main text (either new or promoted from appendix) - each must add significant narrative value
3. Improve visual distribution and aesthetics:
   a. Distribute figures/tables more evenly throughout the paper
   b. Significantly improve the aesthetics of statistical figures

Let me analyze each:

**1. Related Work rewrite:**
I need to understand what AI top-conference Related Work should look like. The key principles are:


- Position my work relative to prior work, not just list papers
- Identify gaps that my work fills
- Organize by themes/dimensions, not by paper
- Each paragraph should have a clear claim about what's missing/different
- End each paragraph with how my work differs/advances

Looking at the current related_work.tex, I have 3 paragraphs covering "Measurement error in causal inference", "Text classifiers and LLM annotation", and "Sensitivity analysis and correction methods". The issue is that it reads like citation stacking—too many references crammed together without a clear narrative arc. I need to restructure this so each paragraph builds a specific argument about what's missing in prior work and how my approach fills that gap.

**2. Adding figures:**

I'm reviewing which figures to include in the main text. I have fig1_dag_taxonomy.pdf for the method section, fig2_bias_distribution.pdf for showing the bias patterns, fig3_asv_ranking.pdf for the experiments, and fig5_f1_vs_bias.pdf for the performance analysis. I also have fig4_binarization.pdf, fig6_asv_heatmap.pdf, and fig7_dirichlet_overlay.pdf available. I need to decide which ones strengthen the narrative and where they fit best without cluttering the paper. So the main text currently has fig1, fig2, fig3, fig5. The appendix has fig4, fig6, fig7.

Candidates to promote to main text:
- fig4_binarization.pdf - shows binarization results
- fig6_asv_heatmap.pdf - ASV heatmap across topologies
- fig7_dirichlet_overlay.pdf - Dirichlet overlay

I need to analyze which would add the most narrative value.

**3. Visual improvements:**
This requires:
a. Redistributing figures/tables more evenly
b. Redrawing figures for better aesthetics

This is a substantial task. Let me think about how to approach this.

Given the scope, I should:
1. First analyze the current state carefully
2. Then plan and execute

For the Related Work, I should load the paper-writing-norms skill and the distilled-writing skill to understand conventions. For the figures, I need to check what's available in the appendix and decide which to promote based on narrative impact. For the aesthetics, I'll need to look at the figure generation scripts and improve them.

Given the user's request to analyze and then execute immediately, I should figure out what I can do directly versus what needs the Main Agent. The Related Work rewrite is straightforward writing I can handle, but deciding which figures to promote and regenerating them will require more planning and potentially additional tools. then either promote from appendix or create new ones. For the visual improvements, I'll need to look at the figure generation scripts and decide whether to edit them myself or hand off the redrawing to Main Agent.

Let me start by checking the paper-writing norms for guidance on the Related Work section, then review which appendix figures should move to the main text. I'll handle the analysis and rewriting directly, but delegate the figure aesthetics work to Main Agent since that'll require running Python scripts on the remote server.

2	name: causal-annot-error-attrib-writing
3	description: 领域蒸馏——LLM标注误差×因果推断论文的叙事策略、图表设计、展示共识（8篇深度精读归纳）
4	---
5	
6	## 叙事类型分布
7	
8	- **突破局限型**：5/8篇主流。Pattern: 工具被广泛使用→揭示被忽视的系统性问题→提出校正。置信度：高。（Gligoric 2025, Baumann 2025, Kim & Garg 2025, TeBlunthuis 2024, Lee 2024）
9	- **统一框架型**：2/8篇，适用于方法论/形式化贡献。（Hernán & Cole 2009, Messing 2026）
10	- **跨界迁移型**：1/8篇，开创性工作连接NLP与causal inference。（Wood-Doughty 2018）
11	
12	## 开篇策略（可直接模仿）
13	
14	**最有效: Adoption-validity gap**（3/8篇，置信度：高）
15	- 先承认工具被广泛采用→揭示"几乎无人验证下游影响"
16	- TeBlunthuis 2024: "85% report metrics but only 1 corrects"
17	- Baumann 2025: 先"transforming research"再"fundamental threat to scientific validity"
18	
19	**Counterintuitive result hook**（Messing 2026）
20	- 更多数据让naive CI更差（45%→12%），打破"more data helps"直觉
21	
22	**Title-as-question**（Gligoric 2025, TeBlunthuis 2024）
23	- "Can Unconfident Annotations Be Used for Confident Conclusions?" 标题即hook
24	
25	## 张力来源（按频率）
26	
27	1. **采用度vs验证度鸿沟**（3/8）："everyone uses it, nobody validates"。置信度：高。
28	2. **假设违反**（2/8）：证明标准假设（独立性/non-differential）不成立。置信度：中。
29	3. **理论-实践差距**（2/8）：校正方法理论正确但实践不稳定。置信度：中。
30	
31	## Figure 1设计模式
32	
33	- **DAG问题示意**：3/8篇。DAG是这个子领域的视觉语言。黑白minimal，starred-node notation。置信度：高。（Hernán 2009, Wood-Doughty 2018, Lee 2024）
34	- **方法流程图**：2/8篇。左到右pipeline，圆角矩形+图标。（Gligoric 2025, Baumann 2025）
35	- **Teaser result**：2/8篇。反直觉结果立刻抓住读者。（Kim & Garg 2025 heatmap, Messing 2026 coverage curve）
36	- **设计共识**：颜色2-3色最小化、面板并排对比、observed vs unobserved清晰标注
37	
38	## 结果可视化共识
39	
40	- **Line plots（convergence/scaling）**：4/8篇主结果图。Log-log尺度标准。适合展示趋势和收敛。置信度：高。
41	- **Multi-panel grid**：3/8篇。极信息密集。Gligoric 2025用18-panel，Baumann 2025用7×5 small-multiples。置信度：高。
42	- **Quantile dot plot**：1/8篇。同时展示bias（中心偏移）和variance（散布）。（TeBlunthuis 2024）
43	- **Gain突出手法**：dashed reference line（coverage=0.95或diagonal）+颜色对比，4/8篇使用。
44	
45	## 表格设计共识
46	
47	- **无表论文**：2/8（理论/框架论文可全用图）。Wood-Doughty 2018, Hernán 2009。
48	- **Checkmark grid（方法×场景）**：TeBlunthuis 2024 Table 1——一目了然展示适用性。极有效。
49	- **Baseline 3-8个，按family分组**（by_category最常见4/6）
50	- **统计报告**：Monte Carlo / bootstrap CI为主（4/8）
51	- **附录标配**：proofs（3/8）、per-task breakdowns（2/8）、prompt细节（2/8）、robustness（4/8）
52	
53	## 图表分工
54	
55	- **结果用图、方法细节用表**：3/8篇。Messing 2026全部结果以figure呈现，tables仅在appendix。
56	- **结果用表、方法概览用图**：2/8篇。Lee 2024所有定量结果在tables。
57	- **Narrative flow共识**：motivation→method→result→ablation（4/8）或problem→framework→evidence→application（2/8）
58	
59	## 可直接模仿的叙事技巧
60	
61	1. **Catastrophic failure前置**：用极端失败（coverage=0%、符号翻转、31-50%错误率）让校正必要性不言自明。3/8篇。置信度：高。（Gligoric 2025, TeBlunthuis 2024, Baumann 2025）
62	2. **Running example贯穿全文**：DAG通过具体案例（smoking→cancer, statin→liver）可理解。2/8篇。置信度：中。（Wood-Doughty 2018, Hernán 2009）
63	3. **Negative result诚实呈现**：方法论领域，负面结果本身就是贡献。2/8篇。置信度：中。（Wood-Doughty 2018矩阵不稳定, Baumann 2025校正权衡）
64	4. **Summary of findings前置**：methods前用bold声明列出结论。1/8但效果显著。（Baumann 2025）
65	5. **Dense multi-panel替代多图**：单张18-panel图承载全部结果。2/8篇。（Gligoric 2025, Kim & Garg 2025）
